(as may become more apparent by shifting
b
by
-NA_{1}
and
A_{3}
by
A_{2}
) and do not appear in the first term describing
D_{3}
, this TQFT is decoupled from the bulk and from
D_{3}
. Thus, as discussed above, we introduce a topological boundary for it along
V_{3}
by imposing Dirichlet boundary conditions for both of its fields and then push this boundary to the junction
\Sigma_{0}
, effectively setting both
b+NA_{1}
and
A_{3}-A_{2}
to be pure
U(1)
gauges at the junction.

In summary, the boundary conditions and boundary term at this fusion junction are

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(D_{3}\times\mathbb{1},D_{3}):

\displaystyle\left.b+NA_{1}\right|_{\Sigma_{0}},\left.A_{3}-A_{2}\right|_{\Sigma_{0}}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\,,

(4.17)

\displaystyle S_{bdry}=-{i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}b\wedge(A_{3}-A_{2})\,.

Other local fusion junctions

All other local fusion junctions involving the triality defect are worked out in Appendix
C
.

4.2.2
Morphism junctions of condensation defects

As explained in Section
2.2
, condensation defects and defects containing a decoupled TQFT that admits topological boundary conditions are Witt equivalent to the identity defect. Demanding well-defined variational principle and gauge invariance at the corresponding morphism junctions fixes the boundary conditions and the extra boundary action at these junctions. In this subsection, we illustrate this using the examples of the morphism junctions between the identity defect and the condensation defects
C_{0}
and
C_{N\over 2}
, and then turn in the next subsection to consider the morphism junction between the defect containing the TQFT
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
and the identity defect.

\text{Hom}(C_{0},\mathbb{1})

Consider a 2-dimensional morphism junction
\Sigma_{0}
between the condensation defect
C_{0}
and the identity defect supported at
V_{lower}
and
V_{upper}
, respectively, as depicted in Figure
11
. We denote by
a
and
b
the defect fields on
C_{0}
and
\mathbb{1}
, respectively.

C_{0}
or
C_{N\over 2}
a
\mathbb{1}
b
\Sigma_{0}
A_{1}
A_{2}
\small{V_{lower}}
\small{V_{upper}}

Figure 11
:
Hom(
C_{0},\mathbb{1}
) junction: The lower part
V_{lower}
supports
C_{0}
(or
C_{N\over 2}
later) and the upper part
V_{upper}
supports
\mathbb{1}
.

The action for this configuration is given by

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{V_{lower}}\mathrm{d}a\,(A_{1}-A_{2})+{i\over 2\pi}\int_{V_{upper}}\mathrm{d}b\,(A_{1}-A_{2})\,.

(4.18)

To find the junction data at
\Sigma_{0}
, we demand gauge invariance and a well-defined variational principle at the junction. First, under the gauge transformations
A_{1,2}\to A_{1,2}+\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1,2}
we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\lambda}S

\displaystyle={i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\left(Na-b\right)\wedge\mathrm{d}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\,.

(4.19)

For small gauge transformations, i.e.
\lambda_{1,2}
are single-valued such that
\oint\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1,2}=0
, one can integrate by parts and obtain
\left.\mathrm{d}(Na-b)\right|_{\Sigma_{0}}=0
for gauge invariance. Then, for the large gauge transformations, i.e
\lambda_{1,2}
have nontrivial winding modes such that
\oint\mathrm{d}\lambda_{1,2}\neq 0
, one can use the Poincaré dual to demand
\Delta_{\lambda}S=i\int_{PD(\mathrm{d}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}),\Sigma_{0})}(Na-b)\overset{!}{\in}2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
for gauge invariance. In other words,
Na-b
is a pure
U(1)
gauge at
\Sigma_{0}
. Let us then write
\left.Na-b\right|_{\Sigma_{0}}=\mathrm{d}\phi
for some compact scalar
\phi
, and get for the variation at
\Sigma_{0}
the following

\left.\delta S\right|_{\Sigma_{0}}={i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathrm{d}\delta\phi\wedge(A_{1}-A_{2})\,.

(4.20)

Now, since
\left.A_{1}-A_{2}\right|_{V_{2}}
is a pure
U(1)
gauge on the worldvolume
V_{2}
of the identity defect which includes the junction
\Sigma_{0}
,
\left.\delta S\right|_{\Sigma_{0}}=0
without needing any extra boundary terms.

In summary, we have the following junction data:

\text{Hom}(C_{0},\mathbb{1}):\quad\left.b-Na,\quad A_{1}-A_{2}\right|_{\Sigma_{0}}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge},\quad S_{bdry}=0\,.

(4.21)

Before moving on, let us describe another approach for arguing (
4.21
). Taking advantage of the topological nature of the configuration in Figure
11
, we turn the upper part
V_{upper}
along which the identity defect lies clockwise until it is fused with the condensation defect, resulting in a single defect along
V_{lower}
that ends topologically at
\Sigma_{0}
. The bulk field
A_{2}
(as well as the defect fields
a
and
b
) then becomes a defect field living only on
V_{lower}
, and the action of this new defect is given by

\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{V_{lower}}\left(Nda-db\right)\left(A_{1}-A_{2}\right)

(4.22)

where the bulk field is now only
A_{1}
. To put this action in a more familiar form, we can denote
c=Na-b
and
\widetilde{A}=A_{1}-A_{2}
and notice that they are both standard
U(1)
gauge fields (with fluxes
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
) which live only on
V_{lower}
(alternatively, we can shift
b
by
Na
and
A_{2}
by
A_{1}
). That is, we obtain a decoupled trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{1}
BF theory on
V_{lower}
with Lagrangian
(i/2\pi)\widetilde{A}dc
. Now, since this defect ends at
\Sigma_{0}
topologically, the boundary conditions for the corresponding worldvolume TQFT should be topological, and as discussed in subsection
4.2.1
they are given by Dirichlet boundary conditions for both of the fields
c
and
\widetilde{A}
. This, in turn, yields Eq. (
4.21
).

Example: Hom(
C_{N\over 2},\mathbb{1}
)

The discussion above straightforwardly applies to the case with a condensation defect with a nontrivial discrete torsion. For example, for
C_{N\over 2}
(for even
N
)

S={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{V_{lower}}\left[(A_{1}-A_{2})\mathrm{d}a+{1\over 2}(A_{1}-A_{2})\mathrm{d}(A_{1}-A_{2})\right]+{i\over 2\pi}\int_{V_{upper}}(A_{1}-A_{2})\mathrm{d}b

(4.23)

and the analysis above can be used to find the following boundary conditions without any boundary term:
17
17

17

The boundary condition for
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is given by the expression below after replacing
\frac{N}{2}
with
k
.

\text{Hom}\left(C_{N\over 2},\mathbb{1}\right):\quad\left.Na+{N\over 2}(A_{1}-A_{2})-b\,,\quad A_{1}-A_{2}\right|_{\Sigma_{0}}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\,,\quad S_{bdry}=0\,.

(4.24)

4.2.3
Morphism junctions of
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}

When a TQFT has a topological boundary condition, there exists a morphism between a defect on which this TQFT lives and the identity, obtained by imposing the topological boundary condition at the morphism junction.
18
18

18

Later, we will also refer to the gapped boundary of
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
as a junction
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}\to\mathbb{1}
. The
\mathbb{1}
here denotes the trivial theory and should not be confused with the identity defect (
4.10
) in the Maxwell theory.
Although a single
U(1)_{N}
Chern-Simons theory does not have a topological boundary condition, a
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
Chern-Simons theory does. One obvious choice is identifying the
U(1)_{N}
and
U(1)_{-N}
gauge fields, including their gauge transformations and variations, at the junction.
19
19

19

This can be thought of as the folding trick—folding the 3-manifold supporting one
U(1)_{N}
theory along a 2-dimensional junction and relabeling the
U(1)_{N}
field with the opposite parity as a
U(1)_{-N}
field.
However, such a boundary condition is not suitable for the
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
theory when the two
U(1)
fields are made of different bulk gauge fields that are independently gauge invariant. Is there a topological boundary condition that respects both
U(1)
gauge symmetries? What is the correct action, including the boundary terms, that is compatible with such a boundary condition?

To answer these questions, we note that the
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
theory

S={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{V}\left(a\mathrm{d}a-b\mathrm{d}b\right)

(4.25)

has two topological boundary conditions for any integer
N
corresponding to condensing the two Lagrangian algebras
20
20

20

When
N=2M
for an odd prime
M
, the given sets are the only Lagrangian algebras.

\mathcal{A}_{\pm}=\left\{l(1,\pm 1)\,|\,l=0,\cdots,N-1\right\}\,.

(4.26)

The gauge-invariant way of condensing
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
is to impose

\left.a\pm b\right|_{\partial V}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}

(4.27)

which are consistent with the bulk on-shell relations which set
a,b
to be flat
U(1)
connections with
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
holonomies.

To impose these boundary conditions, one has to couple the two
U(1)
fields by adding a boundary term
21
21

21

One can add the boundary term with the minus sign instead, but that will turn out to be equivalent using the resulting boundary conditions.
to the action as follows,
22
22

22

On the other hand, the non-gauge invariant condition
\left.a\pm b\right|_{\partial V}=0
can be imposed on the bulk action without a boundary term,
S={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{V}\left(a\mathrm{d}a-b\mathrm{d}b\right)
. This can be seen by the gauge transformation and the variation under these boundary conditions,
\Delta S={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\partial V}\mathrm{d}\lambda\wedge(a\pm b)
and the variation
\left.\delta S\right|_{\partial V}={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\partial V}(a\pm b)\wedge\delta a
, which are trivial.

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{V}\left(a\mathrm{d}a-b\mathrm{d}b\right)+{iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\partial V}a\wedge b

(4.28)

\displaystyle={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{V}(a+b)\wedge\mathrm{d}(a-b)\,.

Then, using the invariance under gauge transformations and an infinitesimal variation on the boundary,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\lambda}S

\displaystyle={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\partial V}\mathrm{d}(-\lambda_{a}+\lambda_{b})\wedge(a+b)\,,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\delta S|_{\partial V}

\displaystyle={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\partial V}(a-b)\wedge\delta(a+b)

and following the analysis around Eq. (
4.19
), the topological boundary conditions that this action can accommodate should satisfy

\left.{N\over 2}(a+b)\right|_{\partial V}=k\mathrm{d}\varphi\,,\qquad\left.a-b\right|_{\partial V}=\text{flat $U(1)$ connections}

(4.30)

for some
k|{N\over 2}
.
23
23

23

This is because under the gauge transformation
a\to a+\mathrm{d}\lambda
,
a={2k\over N}\mathrm{d}\phi\to{2k\over N}\mathrm{d}(\phi+{N\over 2k}\lambda)
and for
\phi+{N\over 2k}\lambda
to be a
2\pi
-periodic boson,
k|{N\over 2}
.
We immediately see that this action is not compatible with odd integer
N
.
24
24

24

We leave finding an action compatible with the gauge invariant topological boundary conditions for odd
N
to a future work.
For even
N
, these boundary conditions always include a condensation of the Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
for
k={N\over 2},1
, given by

\displaystyle\text{Hom($U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N},\mathbb{1}$)}:\quad\left.a\pm b\,\right|_{\partial V}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge},\quad S_{bdry}={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\partial V}a\wedge b\,.

(4.31)

Using the equations of motion
Na,Nb=
pure
U(1)
gauge, this is equivalent to
25
25

25

More specifically,
\left.{N\over 2}(a\pm b)\right|_{\partial V}=\left.{N\over 2}(a\mp b)\pm Nb\,\right|_{\partial V}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}
.

\displaystyle\text{Hom($U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N},\mathbb{1}$)}:

\displaystyle\left.a\pm b,\quad{N\over 2}(a\mp b)\right|_{\partial V}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge},

(4.32)

\displaystyle S_{bdry}={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\partial V}a\wedge b\,.

Alternatively, one can introduce some scalar fields at the junction that cancel the gauge transformations of the
U(1)
fields
a
and
b
and obtain the same boundary conditions by integrating these scalars out, as explained in Section 6.2.2 of
[
13
]
. However, instead of keeping track of these scalars, we will impose Eq. (
4.32
) at the Hom
(U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N},\mathbb{1})
junctions for simplicity.

Example:
\overline{D}_{3}\times(D_{3}\times D_{3})={D}_{3}~U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}\to{D}_{3}

Consider two consecutive fusions,
\overline{D}_{3}\times(D_{3}\times D_{3})
as shown in Figure
12
.

D_{3}
\overline{D}_{3}
\overline{D}_{3}
U(1)_{N},a
U(1)_{-N},b
D_{3}
D_{3}
A_{L}
A_{R}

\beta

Figure 12
:
The
\beta
: Hom(
{D}_{3}\times{D}_{3},\overline{D}_{3}U(1)_{N}
),
\alpha
: Hom(
\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3},D_{3}U(1)_{-N}
) and
\alpha^{\prime}
: Hom(
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N},\mathbb{1}
) junctions.

First, we construct the local fusion junction
D_{3}\times D_{3}
by fusing the two parallel defects along the upper part above the 2d local fusion junction
\beta
. This fused upper part is described by the action

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(D_{3}\times D_{3},\overline{D}_{3}U(1)_{N}):\quad S

\displaystyle=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\beta}\left(A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+\frac{1}{2}A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}\right)

(4.33)

\displaystyle=-\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\beta}\left(A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+\frac{1}{2}A_{R}\mathrm{d}A_{R}\right)\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow\,\overline{D}_{3})}

\displaystyle+\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\beta}(A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})\mathrm{d}(A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow\,U(1)_{N})}

\displaystyle+\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}}\left[A_{I}\wedge A_{R}+(A_{I}-A_{R})\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}\right]\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow\,S_{bdry})}

where we denote by the integral from
\beta
to
\infty
the 3d manifold above the 2d junction
\beta
, and by
\Sigma_{\beta}
the 2d manifold corresponding to the junction
\beta
. The fusion outcome has the expected
\overline{D}_{3}\times U(1)_{N}
along with a boundary term at
\Sigma_{\beta}
. Since
A_{I}^{\prime}
is supported only on the defect above
\beta
, the
U(1)_{N}
is decoupled from the bulk (this can be seen by shifting
A_{I}^{\prime}\to A_{I}^{\prime}-A_{I}-A_{R}
).

Next, we take another copy of
\overline{D}_{3}
and fuse it with the
\overline{D}_{3}
we obtained in the previous fusion in the segment above the
\alpha
junction. The fusion outcome above
\alpha
is described by the action

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3},U(1)_{-N}D_{3}):\quad S=

\displaystyle-\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\alpha}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+\frac{1}{2}A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+\frac{1}{2}A_{R}\mathrm{d}A_{R}\right)

(4.34)

\displaystyle=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\alpha}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+\frac{1}{2}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{L}\right)\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow D_{3})}

\displaystyle-\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\alpha}(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{R})\mathrm{d}(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{R})\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow U(1)_{-N})}

\displaystyle-\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}\left[A_{L}\wedge A_{R}+(A_{L}-A_{R})\wedge A_{I}\right]\,.\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow S_{bdry})}

Since they are decoupled from the bulk, the
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
factors above
\alpha
commute with all other defects, and so it is Witt equivalent to the identity defect on a 3-manifold. Hence, there is a morphism junction between them where the two
U(1)
theories end at
\alpha^{\prime}
in Figure
12
with a topological boundary condition. As explained above, this requires coupling the two theories at the morphism junction by adding a boundary term and choosing a boundary condition out of two (or more) options:

\displaystyle\text{Hom}\left[U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N},\mathbb{1}\right]:

\displaystyle S_{bdry}(\alpha^{\prime})={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}}a\wedge b,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\text{B.c.}\quad\begin{cases}(1)&\left.{N\over 2}(a+b),\quad a-b\right|_{\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\\
(2)&\left.a+b,\quad{N\over 2}(a-b)\right|_{\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\\
\end{cases}

where
a
and
b
denote the
U(1)_{N}
and
U(1)_{-N}
gauge fields which, in our case, are

a=A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R}\,,\quad b=A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{R}\,.

(4.36)

The coefficients
{N\over 2}
in the boundary conditions can be replaced with
N
if we further specify
Na,Nb=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}
.

4.3
Associator 1-morphisms

4.3.1
The method

In four dimensions, the associator 1-morphism between three codimension-1 defects
(a,b,c)
is described by a 2-dimensional TQFT living at the interface between the two different fusions
(a\times b)\times c
and
a\times(b\times c)
. The first three steps in the method used in Section
3.3.1
to compute these associators are still valid in higher-dimensional cases. In particular, the global fusion outcome (Step 3) may contain some decoupled 3d TQFT, either a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory or a trivial TQFT. However, since the junctions (points in Figure
7
) are now 2-manifolds, the last two steps need a slight modification.

Step 4

Interval reduction
: Using the boundary conditions, obtain the 2d theory from the decoupled TQFT in Step 3 after reducing the 3d interval
[\gamma,\beta]
to the 2d manifold
\Sigma_{\beta\gamma}
.

Step 5

Associator
: Evaluate all the boundary actions at the combined junction using the boundary conditions in Step 2 and combine with the 2d theory after the reduction to obtain the associator 1-morphism as

F^{d}_{a,b,c}=(\text{2d theory after reduction})\otimes(\text{2d theory at the combined junctions})\,.

(4.37)

In Step 4, to get the corresponding 2d theory after reducing the interval, we use a prescription based on the spectrum of the local and line operators on the boundary, as explained in Ref.
[
24
]
. The fate of a topological Wilson line
e^{i\oint n_{i}A_{i}}
in 3d after the interval reduction is determined by the boundary conditions of
n_{i}A_{i}
as listed in Table
1
.

Boundary conditions of
n_{i}A_{i}

Operator from
e^{i\oint n_{i}A_{i}}
after reduction

DBC at both ends

Untwisted local operator

NBC at both ends

Line operator

DBC at one and NBC at the other end

Twisted local operator

Table 1
:
Spectrum of the 2d theory based on the boundary conditions. We say
n_{i}A_{i}
has Dirichlet boundary condition (DBC) if
n_{i}A_{i}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}
on the boundary, and
n_{i}A_{i}
has Neumann boundary condition otherwise. Notice that two line operators after reduction arising from
e^{i\oint n_{i}A_{i}}
and
e^{i\oint n_{i}^{\prime}A_{i}}
are identified if
(n_{i}-n_{i}^{\prime})A_{i}
has DBC on at least one boundary.

For the reduction of an Abelian 3d TQFT, if the 2d theory has untwisted local operators charged under line operators, it is a 2d
G
-gauge theory (
G
-SSB phases) where
G
is the group formed by untwisted local operators. In addition, the theory also contains additional 0-form symmetries (besides
G
) which act trivially on local operators but may act non-trivially on twisted local operators via the Lasso action. Thus, strictly speaking, the reduced theory is a 2d
G
-gauge theory enriched by the additional trivially acting symmetry. In the special case where there are no untwisted local operators and
G
is trivial, this is nothing but an SPT phase of those additional symmetries. In the following, we will restrict ourselves to describe only the untwisted local operators and the line operators, and only keep track of the
G
-gauge theory part. We will then refer the reduced theory as a trivial theory when there are no untwisted local operators.

4.3.2
Interval reduction of
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
(even
N
)

In some cases, the global fusion (Step 3) of three triality defects contains a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory with twist
N
(denoted as
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
),

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int_{V}Na^{\prime}db+\frac{N}{2}a^{\prime}da^{\prime}

(4.38)

\displaystyle=\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{V}(a^{\prime}+b)d(a^{\prime}+b)-bdb+\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{\partial V}b\wedge a^{\prime}

which is equivalent to the
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
theory together with the required boundary term, upon redefining
a=a^{\prime}+b
. Thus, the boundary conditions of
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
will apply in this case.

Let us briefly describe the interval reduction of this theory when placed on
V=[\gamma,\beta]\times M_{2}
for the two boundary conditions
\mathcal{A}_{\pm}
discussed in Section
4.2.3
, with the result summarized in Table
2
. Apparently, choosing the same boundary conditions on both ends leads to 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory after reduction. On the other hand, choosing
\mathcal{A}_{+}
on one end and
\mathcal{A}_{-}
on the other leads to 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge theory after reduction. The untwisted local operators have the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
fusion rule and the generator comes from the line
e^{i\oint\frac{N}{2}(a+b)}
. The line operators also have the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
fusion rule and are generated by the reduction of
e^{i\oint a}
, and act non-trivially on the untwisted local operators.

Example: Triality defect associator
\left[F^{D_{3}}_{{D_{3}},D_{3},\overline{D}_{3}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},U(1)_{N}\overline{D}_{3}}

\displaystyle\text{Upper part}:

\displaystyle{\displaystyle D}_{3}\times(D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3})={D}_{3}\times C_{N\over 2}\to D_{3}\times\mathbb{1}=D_{3}\,,

(4.39)

\displaystyle\text{Lower part}:

\displaystyle({D}_{3}\times D_{3})\times\overline{D}_{3}=U(1)_{N}~\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}~D_{3}\to D_{3}\,,

where we have added the morphism junctions
\beta^{\prime}:C_{N\over 2}\to\mathbb{1}

As we did in the 2d case, we first collect the boundary conditions and boundary terms at each junction
\alpha,\beta^{\prime},\beta,\gamma,\delta,\delta^{\prime}
from our junction analyses. Specifically, Eq. (
4.35
) for
\delta^{\prime}:U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}\to\mathbb{1}
, Eq. (
4.34
) for
\delta:D_{3}~U(1)_{-N}\to\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}
, Eq. (
4.33
) for
\gamma:U(1)_{N}~\overline{D}_{3}\to D_{3}\times D_{3}
, Eq. (
C.12
) for

) for

. Then, we shrink the interval between the upper junctions
\alpha,\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta
and combine the boundary conditions and boundary terms there. We repeat analogously for the lower junctions
\gamma,\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
. Putting them all together,

\displaystyle\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}:

\displaystyle N(A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R}),\quad A_{I}-A_{R}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\,,

(4.40)

\displaystyle S(\alpha)={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}A_{L}\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R}),\quad S(\beta^{\prime})={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}\left(A_{R}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}-{1\over 2}A_{I}\wedge A_{R}\right),

\displaystyle\gamma,\delta,\delta^{\prime}:

\displaystyle a_{1}=A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I},\quad a_{2}=A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R},

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(1)&\underbrace{A_{I}-A_{R}}_{=a_{1}-a_{2}},\quad N(A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\\
\text{or }(2)&\underbrace{2(A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})+A_{I}-A_{R}}_{=a_{1}+a_{2}},\quad{N\over 2}(A_{I}-A_{R})=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\end{cases}

\displaystyle S(\gamma)=-{iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\gamma}}\left[A_{L}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}+(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})\wedge A_{I}\right],

\displaystyle S(\delta)={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\delta}}\left(A_{L}\wedge A_{R}+A_{L}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{R}\right),

\displaystyle S(\delta^{\prime})={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\delta^{\prime}}}\left(A_{L}\wedge A_{R}+A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{R}+A_{I}\wedge A_{L}+A_{I}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I}\wedge A_{R}\right)

where
S(\alpha)
denotes the boundary term at the junction
\alpha
(and similarly for the other junctions),
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are the gauge fields of the
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
theory and (1) and (2) are the two possible boundary conditions at the junction
\delta^{\prime}:~U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}\to\mathbb{1}
.

Next, we fuse the three defects
{D}_{3}\times D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}
in the middle (along the interval
[\gamma,\beta]
) by summing their actions,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+{1\over 2}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{L}+A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}+{1\over 2}A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}-A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{R}-{1\over 2}A_{R}\mathrm{d}A_{R}\right)

(4.41)

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+{1\over 2}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{L}\right)\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow D_{3})}

\displaystyle+{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left[(A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})\mathrm{d}(A_{I}-A_{R})+{1\over 2}(A_{I}-A_{R})\mathrm{d}(A_{I}-A_{R})\right]\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N})}

\displaystyle+{iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}-\Sigma_{\gamma}}\left(A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{I}+{1\over 2}A_{R}\wedge A_{I}\right)\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(\leftarrow S_{bdry;global})}

where the second line is the action for
D_{3}
as expected from the fusion algebra, the third line is a decoupled
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory with twist
N
(denoted by
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
) and the last line is the boundary term.

(4.42)

The total boundary term at the (combined) upper junction
\beta
is

\displaystyle S_{bdy,\beta}

\displaystyle=S(\alpha)+S(\beta^{\prime})+\left.S_{global}\right|_{\alpha\beta}

(4.43)

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}(A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})\in 2\pi i\mathbb{Z}

which is trivial by the boundary conditions (
4.40
). Similarly, the total boundary term at the (combined) lower junction
\gamma
is

\displaystyle S_{bdy,\gamma}

\displaystyle=S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{global}\right|_{\gamma\delta}+S(\delta^{\prime})

(4.44)

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\gamma\delta}}(A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I})\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})\in 2\pi i\mathbb{Z}\,,

which is also trivial by the boundary conditions.

Therefore, we are left with a decoupled
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
theory supported on the interval
[\gamma,\beta]
in Eq. (
4.41
). By comparing the action given in (
4.41
) with (
4.38
), we identify that the boundary condition at
\gamma
corresponds to
\mathcal{A}_{-}
, while the two boundary conditions (1) and (2) at
\beta
correspond to
\mathcal{A}_{-}
and
\mathcal{A}_{+}
, respectively.

More concretely, for the boundary condition (1) the upper and lower junctions have the same boundary conditions for the gauge fields
A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R}
and
A_{I}-A_{R}
in the
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
action in (
4.41
), which are written in Table
3
. Following the discussion in Section
4.3.1
, reducing the interval
[\gamma,\beta]
results in a 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory.

). Here, by

Na=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}
and that
e^{i\oint a}
is a

Under the boundary condition (2), on the other hand, the gauge fields have different boundary conditions at the upper and lower junctions, as written in Table

. In this case, we only find

(

).

In summary,

(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}\quad\overset{\text{reducing interval}}{\longrightarrow}\quad\begin{cases}(1):&\mathbb{Z}_{N}\text{ gauge theory}\\
(2):&\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ gauge theory}\,\,\,.\end{cases}

(4.45)

Overall, we obtained that the boundary terms do not contribute to this associator 1-morphism (see Eqs. (
4.43
) and (
4.44
)), and hence it is given only by the 2d theory obtained by reducing the
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
theory on the interval
[\gamma,\beta]
, which we found in (
4.45
). That is,

\left[F^{D_{3}}_{D_{3},D_{3},\overline{D}_{3}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},U(1)_{N}\overline{D}_{3}}=\mathbb{Z}_{N}\text{ gauge theory}\quad\text{or}\quad\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ gauge theory}

(4.46)

where the two possibilities correspond to the two options for the boundary conditions at the junction
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}\to\mathbb{1}
.

27

27

There are only two possible topological boundary conditions for the
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
theory since we have restricted here to
N=2\times(\textrm{odd prime})
.

Other associators

We compute all the other associators involving the triality defect
D_{3}
and its orientation reversal
\overline{D}_{3}
in Appendix
C.2
. The results are listed in Table
5
.

Associators corresponding to various combinations of the

and with trivial Witt elements (i.e. the identity defect) selected at the fusion junctions. The two different possibilities for the associator of the last two combinations correspond to the two types of topological boundary conditions of the
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
theory that appears in the fusion process. The other possible four associators are related to those in the table by orientation reversal.

For general even values of
N
, we expect to have more topological boundary conditions at the morphism junction
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}\to\mathbb{1}
, corresponding to condensing nonanomalous
\mathbb{Z}_{2k}

, which results in
\mathbb{Z}_{2k}

In the next section, we introduce a group-theoretical construction for the triality defects for certain odd values of
N
and compute the above associators, which agree with the ones in Table
5
except for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge theory in the last column which becomes the trivial theory.
28
28

28

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge theory in these associators results from the dimensional reduction of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory with twist
N
,
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
, on the interval. Due to the non-trivial twist
N
, this theory has an anomaly for even
N
, and hence the 2d theory obtained after the dimensional reduction cannot be trivial. On the other hand, it is not anomalous for odd
N
and indeed
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}\simeq(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{0}
for such
N
, which allows for a trivial TQFT after the dimensional reduction.

Lastly, in Appendix
E
, we also provide an example for the computation of a duality defect associator,
[F_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}^{D_{2}}]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}
, using the Lagrangian method and the group-theoretical approach and list the results for the other duality associators, which agree between the two methods.

5
Triality defects in Maxwell theory using group theoretical construction

In this section, we describe a complementary computation of the associativity map for triality defects that admit certain group-theoretical constructions for odd
N
. Recall that the fusion rules are

D_{3}\times D_{3}=SU(N)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3}\;\,,\;\,\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=SU(N)_{1}D_{3}\;\,,\;\,\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}=D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=C_{0}~.

(5.1)

Here, CS theory
SU(N)_{\pm 1}
is a bosonic TQFT containing
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
Abelian anyons. Denoting the generating line as
a
, the topological spin
h(a^{k})
of
a^{k}
and the braiding phase
B(a^{k},a^{k^{\prime}})
are
29
29

29

This can be directly computed using
[
25
]
, or use the fact that
SU(N)_{1}
is the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{N,N-1}

[
26
,
27
]
and
\frac{k(N-k)}{2N}=\frac{N-1}{2N}k^{2}\mod 1
.

h(a^{k})=\pm\frac{k(N-k)}{2N}\mod 1~,\quad B(a^{k},a^{k^{\prime}})=e^{\mp\frac{2\pi i}{N}kk^{\prime}}~,\quad\text{for}\quad SU(N)_{\pm 1}~.

(5.2)

In the case of the spin theory, there is an additional transparent fermion
\psi
.
SU(N)_{-1}\boxtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\psi}
now contains
\mathbb{Z}_{2N}=\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{a}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\psi}
Abelian anyons (as
N
is odd), and is generated by the anyon
b=a\psi
. The topological spin and the braiding phases are

h(b^{k})=\frac{k^{2}}{2N}~,\quad B(b^{k},b^{k^{\prime}})=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}kk^{\prime}}~,

(5.3)

which matches the spectrum of the
U(1)_{N}
CS theory. This relation is known as the level-rank duality
[
28
]
. For the following, we will assume that we work with bosonic theory for simplicity; although the result holds for the spin theory as well since the additional fermion does not have any essential effect due to its transparent nature.

5.1
Group-theoretical construction of triality defects

As first pointed out in
[
12
]
, a non-invertible duality defect in 4d theory can be constructed from gauging a certain 1-form symmetry in a 2-group symmetry with a certain mixed anomaly. In
[
14
]
, a group-theoretical construction for certain duality and triality defects is provided. Let us first explain in detail the triality defects. Consider a 4d QFT
\mathcal{X}
with
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
(where
N
is odd) 1-form symmetry, and its partition function is invariant under the following order-
3
twisted gauging

Z_{\mathcal{X}}[A^{(2)}]=\sum_{a^{(2)}\in H^{2}(X_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{N})}Z_{\mathcal{X}}[a^{(2)}]\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{N}\frac{N+1}{2}\int a^{(2)}\cup a^{(2)}+\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int a^{(2)}\cup A^{(2)}\right)~.

(5.4)

One can define a new theory
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
from
\mathcal{X}
by twisted gauging the 1-form symmetry

Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[A^{(2)}]=\sum_{a^{(2)}\in H^{2}(X_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{N})}Z_{\mathcal{X}}[a^{(2)}]\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{N}p^{\prime}\int a^{(2)}\cup a^{(2)}+\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int a^{(2)}\cup A^{(2)}\right)~.

(5.5)

When the odd
N
is such that the following equation of
p^{\prime}
admits solutions

(2p^{\prime})^{2}-2p^{\prime}+1=0\mod N~,

(5.6)

the partition function of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
satisfies

Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[A^{(2)}]=Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}A^{(2)}]\exp\left(\frac{2\pi i}{N}\left(-\frac{N+1}{2}(2p^{\prime})^{-1}\right)\int A^{(2)}\cup A^{(2)}\right)~,

(5.7)

where
(2p^{\prime})^{-1}
denotes the mod-
N
inverse of
(2p^{\prime})
. By (
5.6
), we can choose
(2p^{\prime})^{-1}=1-2p^{\prime}
and we will also choose
(2p^{\prime})
to be odd such that
(2p^{\prime})^{-1}=1-2p^{\prime}
is even.

Using
(-2p^{\prime})^{3}=1
from (
5.6
), one can see that the triality twisted gauging in
\mathcal{X}
is mapped to an invertible
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
automorphism of the dual 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
. Together, they form a non-Abelian 2-group symmetry
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
. The additional phase indicates the mixed anomaly between the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
symmetries. Due to this anomaly, the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
-symmetry operator
\mathbb{D}_{3}
is not gauge invariant after gauging the dual 1-form symmetry to regain the theory
\mathcal{X}
. To acquire a gauge invariant codim-1 operator, one can stack a TQFT coupled to the gauge field
a^{(2)}
of the dual symmetry to cancel the anomaly. There is a canonical choice known as the minimal TQFT.

Let us recall some basic properties of the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{N,p}
in the special case where
N
is odd and
p
is even and coprime with
N

[
26
]
. The non-spin version of this TQFT contains
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
Abelian anyons
a^{s}
(where
a^{N}=1
) with the topological spin

h(a^{s})=\frac{ps^{2}}{2N}\mod 1~.

(5.8)

The braiding between any two generic Abelian anyons
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
is

B(a_{1},a_{2})=e^{2\pi i[h(a_{1}a_{2})-h(a_{1})-h(a_{2})]}~.

(5.9)

The minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{N,p}
can be used to cancel the anomaly of the 1-form symmetry captured by the 4d SPT
[
26
,
27
]

-\frac{2\pi i}{N}\frac{N+1}{2}p\int B^{(2)}\cup B^{(2)}~.

(5.10)

Therefore,
D_{3}=\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}\left[a^{(2)}\right]
30
30

30

Notice that we also use the letter
a
to denote Abelian anyons. Whenever we use
a^{(n)}
to refer to gauge fields, we will always add the superscript
{(n)}
to denote its degree so the readers can distinguish between
n
-form gauge fields
a^{(n)}
and anyons
a
’s.
with an even choice of
(2p^{\prime})^{-1}
is gauge invariant in
\mathcal{X}
, but becomes non-invertible because of
\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}[a^{(2)}]
.

The orientation reversal of
\overline{D}_{3}
is

\overline{D}_{3}=\mathcal{A}^{N,-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}[a^{(2)}]\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}~,

(5.11)

where the anomaly of the minimal TQFT is flipped because of the orientation reversal.

It is instructive to check the fusion rule explicitly to highlight two subtleties compared to the simple case in
[
12
]
. First, the non-Abelian 2-group here does not commute with the minimal TQFT coupled to
a^{(2)}
:

\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,p}[a^{(2)}]=\mathcal{A}^{N,p}[-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}a^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}_{3}~.

(5.12)

Notice that here we interpret the action of
\mathbb{D}_{3}
as changing how the bulk field
a^{(2)}
couples to the anyons in the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{N,p}
, and
\mathbb{D}_{3}
itself does not act on the lines in
\mathcal{A}^{N,p}
. Second, for generic minimal TQFTs, it is not easy to manipulate the Lagrangian to prove various relations, and we will demonstrate how to do so by directly analyzing the anyon spectrum.

For instance, let’s consider the fusion of
D_{3}\times D_{3}
. We find

\displaystyle D_{3}\times D_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{1}}[a^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{2}}[a^{(2)}]

(5.13)

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{1}}[-(2p^{\prime})a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{2}}[a^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\left[SU(N)_{-1}\right]_{a_{1}a_{2}^{2p^{\prime}}}\,\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}\mathcal{A}_{a_{1}^{-2p^{\prime}}a_{2}}^{N,-N+1}[a^{(2)}]\equiv SU(N)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3}~.

Here, the subscript under each TQFT denotes its generating line. In the second equal sign, we used

\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}[a^{(2)}]=\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}[-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}a^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}_{3}~,

(5.14)

followed from the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{(0)}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
. To show the third step, one can consider the anyon spectrum of
\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{1}}[-(2p^{\prime})a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{2}}[a^{(2)}]
. We can redefine the generating lines as

b_{1}=a_{1}^{-(2p^{\prime})}a_{2}~,\quad b_{2}=a_{1}a_{2}^{(2p^{\prime})}~.

(5.15)

Furthermore, because
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
braid trivially with each other, the theory is factorized into two theories generated by
b_{i}
, respectively. First, the bulk 2-form gauge field
a^{(2)}
couples to the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
line
b_{1}
which has topological spin

\displaystyle h(a_{1}^{-2p^{\prime}}a_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}{2N}\left((-2p^{\prime})^{2}+1\right)\mod 1

(5.16)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}{2N}\left(2p^{\prime}\right)\mod 1

\displaystyle=\frac{-N+1}{2N}\mod 1

where in the last step we used
(2p^{\prime})^{-1}(2p^{\prime})=-N+1\mod 2N
(as
(2p^{\prime})^{-1}(2p^{\prime})
is even by our choice). Thus, we see that the theory generated by
b_{1}
is the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{N,-N+1}_{b_{1}}[a^{(2)}]
. To see that
\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,-N+1}_{b_{1}}[a^{(2)}]
is indeed
\overline{D}_{3}
given in (
5.11
), one uses first the commutation relation (
5.14
) and then the certain identity of minimal TQFT

\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,-N+1}_{b_{1}}[a^{(2)}]=\mathcal{A}^{N,-N+1}_{b_{1}}[-(2p^{\prime})a^{(2)}]\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}=\mathcal{A}^{N,-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{c}[a^{(2)}]\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}~,

(5.17)

where
c=b_{1}^{-2p^{\prime}}
. The last step can be shown as follows:
a^{(2)}
couples to the line
b_{1}^{-2p^{\prime}}
with topological spin

h(c)=h(b_{1}^{-2p^{\prime}})=\frac{-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}{2N}\mod 1~,

(5.18)

thus if we consider a change of variable
c=b_{1}^{-2p^{\prime}}=a_{1}^{-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}a_{2}^{-2p^{\prime}}
, we see that the minimal TQFT can be rewritten as
\mathcal{A}^{N,-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{c}[a^{(2)}]
.

On the other hand, the anyons
b_{2}^{k}
form
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and their topological spins are

h(b_{2}^{k})=\frac{-N+1}{2N}k^{2}\mod 1=-\frac{k(N-k)}{2N}\mod 1~,

(5.19)

thus they give rise to the theory
SU(N)_{-1}
(
5.2
). Combining the two discussions, we recover the fusion rule (
5.13
).

As another example, let’s consider
D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}
:

D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{1}}[a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{N,-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{2}}[a^{(2)}]\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}=\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{C}_{0}\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}=\mathcal{C}_{0}~.

(5.20)

The key step here is to show that
\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{1}}[a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{N,-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{2}}[a^{(2)}]
is the condensation defect
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. We can reparameterize the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}\times\mathbb{Z}_{N}
anyons with the following two generators

b_{1}=(a_{1}a_{2}^{-1})^{-\frac{N+1}{2}p^{\prime}}~,\quad b_{2}=a_{1}a_{2}~.

(5.21)

The new generating lines
b_{i}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
bosons and have mutual braiding

B(b_{1},b_{2})=e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}}~.

(5.22)

This implies that
b_{i}
’s generate a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory with no twist and
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
correspond to the electric line and the magnetic line respectively. Thus, we can write down an action for it

S=\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int b^{(1)}\cup\delta\hat{b}^{(1)}+\hat{b}^{(1)}\cup a^{(2)}~,

(5.23)

where the coupling
\hat{b}^{(1)}\cup a^{(2)}
comes from the fact that
a^{(2)}
couples to the line
b_{2}
. This is nothing but a condensation defect
[
12
]
, as

\displaystyle\int[Db^{(1)}][D\hat{b}^{(1)}]e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int b^{(1)}\cup\delta\hat{b}^{(1)}+\hat{b}^{(1)}\cup a^{(2)}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|H^{1}(X_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{N})|}}\sum_{\hat{b}^{(1)}\in H^{1}(X_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{N})}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int b^{(1)}\cup a^{(2)}}

(5.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|H^{1}(X_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{N})|}}\sum_{\sigma\in H_{2}(X_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{N})}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint_{\sigma}a^{(2)}}\equiv\mathcal{C}_{0}~,

where we first integrate out
b^{(1)}
to set
\hat{b}^{(1)}
flat and then re-express the sum using the Poincaré dual
\sigma\in H_{2}(X_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{N})
of
\hat{b}^{(1)}\in H^{1}(X_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{N})
.

It is interesting to notice that the magnetic line
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint_{\gamma}\hat{b}^{(1)}}
is topological and corresponds to higher quantum symmetry. The electric line
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint_{\gamma}b^{(1)}}
, however, is no longer a genuine line operator as
b^{(1)}
due to the modified gauge transformation
b^{(1)}\rightarrow b^{(1)}-\lambda^{(1)}_{a}
under the gauge transformation of
a^{(2)}\rightarrow a^{(2)}+\delta\lambda_{a}^{(1)}
. This leads to two interesting configurations. First, the bulk 1-form symmetry operator
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int a^{(2)}}
can terminate topologically on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
(as the 1-form symmetry is gauged on
\mathcal{C}_{0}
). This is realized by dressing
e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint_{\gamma}b^{(1)}}
on the intersection of
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int a^{(2)}}
with
\mathcal{C}_{0}
to ensure the gauge invariance. Second, a non-genuine line operator
L(\partial\sigma)
bounding the 1-form symmetry operator
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int_{\sigma}a^{(2)}}
(so that the combined
L(\partial\sigma)e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\int_{\sigma}a^{(2)}}
is gauge invariant) in the bulk will become a genuine line when pushing to
\mathcal{C}_{0}
because the 1-form symmetry is gauged. This is described by the gauge invariant line
L(\partial\sigma)e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint_{\partial\sigma}b^{(1)}}
. Furthermore,
L(\partial\sigma)e^{-\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint_{\partial\sigma}b^{(1)}}
is charged under the higher quantum symmetry generated by the magnetic line. Comparing with the description of the Lagrangian condensation defect in Maxwell theory, we see that the higher quantum symmetry
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint_{\gamma}\hat{b}^{(1)}}
is matched by
e^{i\oint A_{L}-A_{R}}
there; and the genuine line operator
e^{i\oint a}
on the condensation defect in the Lagrangian description is an example of the bulk non-genuine line operator which becomes genuine on the condensation defect.

5.2
Overview of anyon condensations and fusion of domain walls

Following the general discussion in Section
2.2
, to compute the associator for the three defects
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\mathcal{D}_{3}
(where
\mathcal{D}_{i}
are triality defects dressed with corresponding minimal TQFT), we need to evaluate the following diagram

where
x=0,1,2
depending
\mathcal{D}_{i}
,
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
are the topological interfaces between
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
respectively. The
F
-symbol here is then acquired by fusing the two interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{+}
and
\mathcal{I}_{-}
.

The simplification of computing associators in this group-theoretical construction is that everything can be phrased in the language of 3d TQFT, i.e. unitary modular tensor categories (UMTC). Below, we will first include a summary of the results, and will include a detailed description on how to use anyon condensation theory to acquire this in Appendix
D
.

First, the TQFT
\mathcal{T}
is described by a UMTC and the interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{\pm},\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
are acquired by anyon condensations. Following
[
2
]
, we use
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
to denote the group of condensed anyons. Then, we want to describe the topological line operator contents in the following configuration

(5.27)

and they are summarized as follows in Table
6
.

Line Operators

Fusion rules

\mathcal{T}_{+}

L_{b}^{+}:b\in\mathcal{T}/\mathbb{A}_{+}
and
B(b,a)=1\,\,\forall a\in\mathbb{A}_{+}

\mathcal{T}_{+}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}_{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{+}:L_{b}^{+}\times L_{b^{\prime}}^{+}=L_{bb^{\prime}}^{+}

\mathcal{T}_{-}

L_{b}^{-}:b\in\mathcal{T}/\mathbb{A}_{-}
and
B(b,a)=1\,\,\forall a\in\mathbb{A}_{-}

\mathcal{T}_{-}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}_{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}_{-}:L_{b}^{-}\times L_{b^{\prime}}^{-}=L_{bb^{\prime}}^{-}

\mathcal{I}_{+}

\ell^{+}_{b},\quad b\in\mathcal{T}/\mathbb{A}_{+}

\mathcal{T}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{+}:b\otimes\ell^{+}_{b^{\prime}}=\ell_{bb^{\prime}}^{+}

\mathcal{I}_{+}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{+}:\ell^{+}_{b}\otimes\ell^{+}_{b^{\prime}}=\ell_{bb^{\prime}}^{+}

\mathcal{I}_{+}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}_{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{+}:\ell^{+}_{b}\otimes L^{+}_{b^{\prime}}=\ell^{+}_{bb^{\prime}}

\mathcal{I}_{-}

\ell^{-}_{b},\quad b\in\mathcal{T}/\mathbb{A}_{-}

\mathcal{I}_{-}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{-}:\ell^{-}_{b}\otimes b^{\prime}=\ell_{bb^{\prime}}^{-}

\mathcal{I}_{-}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{-}:\ell^{-}_{b}\otimes\ell^{-}_{b^{\prime}}=\ell_{bb^{\prime}}^{-}

\mathcal{T}_{-}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{-}:L^{-}_{b}\otimes\ell^{-}_{b^{\prime}}=\ell^{-}_{bb^{\prime}}

Table 6
:
The operator contents and fusion rules in (
5.27
). When list the operator contents, by
b\in\mathcal{T}/\mathbb{A}_{+}
in
\mathcal{I}_{+}
for instance, we mean the line
\ell_{b}^{+}
is the same line as
\ell_{b^{\prime}}^{+}
if
b^{-1}b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{A}_{+}
. Notice that while we only list the fusion rules, the line operators in
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
actually form a modular tensor category respectively and the braiding structure is inherent from
\mathcal{T}
. The domain walls
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
are fusion categories, but they also carry actions of the theories on both sides, as captured by the additional fusion rules given above.

To acquire the
F
-symbol, we consider fusing the domain walls
\mathcal{I}_{+}
and
\mathcal{I}_{-}
which again can be computed using the anyon condensation theory. The outcome is a domain wall
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
between
\mathcal{T}_{-}
and
\mathcal{T}_{+}
, which we identify as the
F
-symbol. On the fused domain wall, besides the topological line operators, generically it may also admit local operators, which link non-trivially with the topological line operators on the wall. The result is summarized as follows in Table
7
and details can be found in Appendix
D
.

Line operators

\ell^{-+}_{b},\quad b\in\mathbb{A}_{-}\text{\textbackslash}\mathcal{T}/\mathbb{A}_{+}

\mathcal{I}_{-+}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{-+}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{-+}:\ell^{-+}_{b}\otimes\ell^{-+}_{b^{\prime}}=\ell^{-+}_{bb^{\prime}}

\mathcal{I}_{-+}\boxtimes\mathcal{T}_{+}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{-+}:L_{b}^{-}\otimes\ell^{-+}_{b^{\prime}}=\ell^{-+}_{bb^{\prime}}

\mathcal{T}_{-}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{-+}\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{-+}:\ell^{-+}_{b}\otimes L_{b^{\prime}}^{+}=\ell^{-+}_{bb^{\prime}}

Local operators

V_{a},\quad a\in\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}

B(V_{a},\ell^{+-}_{b})=B(a,b)

Table 7
:
The topological operator contents on the fused wall
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
. Generically, there is non-trivial braiding between the local operators and the line operators inherent from the braiding in
\mathcal{T}
.

Finally, one also needs to remember to keep track if the resulting 2d interface
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
couples to the bulk gauge field
a^{(2)}
. As we will see, this will change the physical meaning of the 2d interface.

5.3
Local fusion junctions

In this subsection, we list the local fusion junctions where the defects are given by the simplest representatives in the equivalence classes. We will demonstrate the construction by an example, and list all other results afterwards.

D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=\mathcal{C}_{0}

\mathcal{C}_{0}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~,

(5.30)

is acquired by condensing the anyon coupled to the bulk gauge field
a^{(2)}
. Applying this to the local junction (
5.28
), in the global fusion

D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=\mathcal{A}^{N,(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{1}}[-(2p^{\prime})a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{N,-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}}_{a_{2}}[-(2p^{\prime})a^{(2)}]=(\mathcal{Z}_{7})_{0}[-(2p^{\prime})a^{(2)}]\equiv\mathcal{C}_{0}~,

(5.31)

the bulk gauge field
a^{(2)}
couples to the anyon
b_{2}=(a_{1}a_{2})^{-2p^{\prime}}
. We thus conclude that the condensed anyon
\mathbb{A}=\langle(a_{1}a_{2})^{-2p^{\prime}}\rangle=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle
.

The other local fusion junctions can be constructed similarly, and we summarize the results in Table
8
.

Table 8
:
The list of local fusion junctions that requires anyon condensations to construct. We use the subscript
a
in
[D_{3}]_{a}
and
[\overline{D}_{3}]_{a}
to denote the generating anyons in the stacked minimal TQFT and the presentation of
\overline{D}_{3}
in (
5.11
) is used. We also the junctions where no anyon is condensed to make the change of anyon generators explicit. In the
4
-th and the last junctions, we use the anyon condensation to remove
[SU(N)_{1}]_{a}\times[SU(N)_{-1}]_{b}
. Since
N
is odd, it is straightforward to check this is the same as a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-gauge theory with no twist (where
ab
generates the electric lines and
ab^{-1}
generates the magnetic lines). Generically there will be more than two choices of condensable anyons we provided depending on
N
; here, we only list the two which exists for all
N
.

5.4
Associators for triality defects

With the above tools we can now compute the associators of the triality defect admitting the above group-theoretical construction. For simplicity, we will consider the simplest special case where
N=7
, and it’s straightforward to generalize our approach for generic allowed
N
. We will choose

2p^{\prime}=3~,\quad(2p^{\prime})^{-1}=1-2p^{\prime}=-2~,\quad-(2p^{\prime})^{-1}=2~,

(5.32)

and the triality defects are given by

D_{3}=\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]~,\quad\overline{D}_{3}=\mathcal{A}^{7,2}[a^{(2)}]\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}~.

(5.33)

In the following, we will demonstrate the computation through the following four examples:

D_{3}\times D_{3}\times D_{3}~,\quad D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}~,\quad\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}\times D_{3}~,\quad D_{3}\times D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}~,

(5.34)

and the other four are related to the above by orientation reversal.

\mathbf{\left[F_{D_{3},{D}_{3},D_{3}}^{D_{3}}\right]_{SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3},SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3}}}

Let’s consider the
F
-symbol for
D_{3}\times D_{3}\times D_{3}
. Evaluating the global fusion in two different ways, we find

\displaystyle(D_{3}\times D_{3})\times D_{3}=SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}=SU(7)_{-1}\mathcal{C}_{0}\rightarrow SU(7)_{-1}~,

(5.35)

\displaystyle D_{3}\times(D_{3}\times D_{3})=D_{3}\times(SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3})=SU(7)_{-1}\mathcal{C}_{0}\rightarrow SU(7)_{-1}~.

Thus, the corresponding diagram is

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-15.0181pt}{62.43852pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

(5.36)

Here, the theory
\mathcal{T}
is extracted using (
5.14
)

\displaystyle D_{3}\times D_{3}\times D_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{1}}[a^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{2}}[a^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{3}}[a^{(2)}]

(5.37)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{1}}[a^{(2)}]\overline{D}_{3}\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{2}}[4a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{3}}[a^{(2)}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{1}}[2a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{2}}[4a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{3}}[a^{(2)}]~.

To find that the condensed anyons
\mathbb{A}_{+}
, let’s follow the fusion tree

D_{3}\times(D_{3}\times D_{3})=D_{3}\times(SU(N)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3})=SU(N)_{-1}\mathcal{C}_{0}\rightarrow SU(N)_{-1}~.

(5.38)

In the first junction,
(D_{3}\times D_{3})=U(1)_{N}\mathcal{A}^{7,2}[a^{(2)}]\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}
, as discussed previously, the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{7,2}[a^{(2)}]
is generated by the anyon
a_{2}^{4}a_{3}
to which the gauge field
a^{(2)}
couples. No anyon condensation happens here. Next, the second junction is simply the local junction between
D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}
and the
\mathbb{1}
, and as shown previously, the anyon that couples to the gauge field
a^{(2)}
is condensed on this interface; therefore
\mathbb{A}_{+}=\langle a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{4}a_{3}\rangle
. By the similar analysis, one can show that
\mathbb{A}_{-}=\langle a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{4}a_{3}\rangle
.

Using the result in Table
7
, we see that both
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
are
SU(7)_{-1}
’s generated by
L_{a_{2}a_{3}^{3}}^{\pm}
respectively. The fused interface
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
contains topological line operators factorized into
\mathbb{Z}_{7}\times\mathbb{Z}_{7}
, where one
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
factor corresponds to the unconfined lines in
SU(7)_{-1}
and the other
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
factor corresponds to topological lines confined on the interface. By checking the fusion rule between the
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}=SU(7)_{-1}
and the
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
unconfined sector, we see that the unconfined
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
sector forms the identity interface between
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
theories.

Furthermore, the interface also contains
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
topological vertex operators labeled by
V_{a}
where
a\in\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}=\langle a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{4}a_{3}\rangle
. From the braiding relation between the vertex operators and the confined lines, we see that they form a 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
TQFT on the interface. However, this TQFT actually couples to the bulk gauge field
a^{(2)}
through the vertex operator, thus an effective action is given by

S=\frac{2\pi i}{7}\int\left(b^{(1)}\cup\delta\phi^{(0)}+\phi^{(0)}a^{(2)}\right)~.

(5.39)

By the similar multiplication where we integrate out
b^{(1)}\in C^{1}(M_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{7})
, we see that this is nothing but a 2d condensation defect of the bulk
\mathbb{Z}_{7}^{(1)}
symmetry (which is actually just a projector). Hence, we conclude in this case that the
F
-symbol interface is given by the identity interface of the
SU(7)_{-1}
TQFT stacked with a 2d condensation defect of the bulk
\mathbb{Z}_{7}^{(1)}
symmetry.

Let us briefly comment on the operator spectrum. First,
e^{\frac{2\pi ik}{N}\phi^{(0)}}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
topological local operators (arising from the higher quantum symmetry line
e^{\frac{2\pi ik}{N}\int a^{(1)}}
stretching between two boundaries) and they can be interpreted as the 1-form higher quantum symmetry on the 2d condensation defect. The gauge field
b^{(1)}
again has the modified gauge transformation
b^{(1)}\rightarrow b^{(1)}-\lambda_{a}^{(1)}
under the
a^{(2)}
gauge transformation
a^{(2)}\rightarrow a^{(2)}+\delta\lambda_{a}^{(1)}
. Therefore,
e^{\frac{2\pi i}{N}\oint b^{(1)}}
plays the same role as the 3d condensation defect, namely to ensure the non-genuine line bounding the bulk 1-form
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry becomes genuine on the 2d condensation defect. Notice that this matches straightforwardly with the same discussion in the Lagrangian formalism in Appendix
5.4
.

To summarize, we find

\mathbf{\left[F_{D_{3},{D}_{3},D_{3}}^{D_{3}}\right]_{SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3},SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3}}}=\text{2d condensation defect of $\mathbb{Z}_{7}$ 1-form symmetry}~.

(5.40)

\mathbf{\left[F_{D_{3},\overline{D}_{3},D_{3}}^{D_{3}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}}

D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}
. In this case, the diagram can be summarized as

(5.41)

The theory
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{1}}[a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{7,2}_{a_{2}}[4a^{(2)}]\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}_{a_{3}}[a^{(2)}]
is extracted similarly using (
5.14
) with the presentation of
\overline{D}_{3}=\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]\overline{\mathbb{D}}_{3}
. From Table
8
, we find

\mathbb{A}_{-}=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle~,\quad\mathbb{A}_{+}=\langle a_{2}a_{3}\rangle~.

(5.42)

In this case, the fused domain wall
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
contains only topological lines labeled by
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
and no local operators as
\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}
is trivial. The theory
\mathcal{T}_{+}=\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]
is generated by the anyon
L^{+}_{a_{1}}
and the theory
\mathcal{T}_{-}=\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]
is generated by the anyon
L^{-}_{a_{3}}
. Because both
L^{+}_{a_{1}}
and
L^{-}_{a_{3}}
couple to the bulk gauge field
a^{(2)}
respectively, it is natural to identify the two lines. On the other hand, pushing
L^{+}_{a_{1}}
and
L^{-}_{a_{3}}
to the interface, they become
\ell^{-+}_{a_{1}}
and
\ell^{-+}_{a_{3}}
respectively. But because
a_{1}=a_{1}(a_{1}a_{2})^{-1}(a_{2}a_{3})
, we see that

\ell^{-+}_{a_{1}}=\ell^{-+}_{a_{1}(a_{1}a_{2})^{-1}(a_{2}a_{3})}=\ell^{-+}_{a_{3}}~,

(5.43)

therefore pushing
L^{+}_{a_{1}}
and
L^{-}_{a_{3}}
leads to the identical line on the interface. Thus, we conclude that the
F
-symbol interface here is the identity interface between triality defect
D_{3}
and
D_{3}
.

\mathbf{\left[F_{\overline{D}_{3},{D}_{3},D_{3}}^{D_{3}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3}}}

This component of the associator relates the following two fusion channels:

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{3}\times(D_{3}\times D_{3})

\displaystyle=\overline{D}_{3}\times(SU(7)_{-1}\,\overline{D}_{3})=(SU(7)_{1}\times SU(7)_{-1})D_{3}\to D_{3}~,

(5.44)

\displaystyle(\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3})\times D_{3}

\displaystyle=C_{0}\times D_{3}\to D_{3}~,

and the corresponding diagram can be worked out by referring to Table

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~,

(5.45)

from which we conclude

\mathbb{A}_{-}=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle~,\quad\mathbb{A}_{+,\text{I}}=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle~,\quad\mathbb{A}_{+,\text{II}}=\langle a_{1}^{3}a_{2}a_{3}\rangle~,

(5.46)

where for
\mathbb{A}_{+}
we have two choices. For the choice of
\mathbb{A}_{+,\text{I}}
, the interface
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
contains vertex operators labeled by
\mathbb{A}_{+,\text{I}}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{7}
and confined
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
topological lines generated by
\ell^{-+}_{a_{1}a_{2}^{-1}}
. Together they form a decoupled 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
-gauge theory. One can further check from the fusion rules between unconfined lines and the bulk lines that the interface
\mathcal{I}_{-+,\text{I}}
is the identity interface in the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]
plus the decoupled 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
gauge theory.

For the choice of
\mathbb{A}_{+,\text{II}}
, there are no confined lines and no vertex operators on the interface
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
. And it is straightforward to check that the
\mathcal{I}_{-+,\text{II}}
is simply the identity interface in the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]
.

In summary,

\mathbf{\left[F_{\overline{D}_{3},{D}_{3},D_{3}}^{D_{3}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3}}}=\text{decoupled $\mathbb{Z}_{7}$ gauge theory \quad or \quad trivial TQFT}~.

(5.47)

\mathbf{\left[F_{D_{3},{D}_{3},\overline{D}_{3}}^{D_{3}}\right]_{SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3},\mathbb{1}}}

This component of the associator relates the two following fusions.

\displaystyle D_{3}\times(D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3})

\displaystyle=D_{3}\times C_{0}\to D_{3}~,

(5.48)

\displaystyle(D_{3}\times D_{3})\times\overline{D}_{3}

\displaystyle=SU(7)_{-1}\,\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=SU(7)_{-1}\times SU(7)_{1}\,{D}_{3}\to D_{3}~,

and the corresponding diagram can be worked out by referring to Table
8
:

(5.49)

from which we conclude

\mathbb{A}_{-,\text{I}}=\langle a_{2}a_{3}\rangle~,\quad\mathbb{A}_{-,\text{II}}=\langle a_{1}^{-2}a_{2}^{2}a_{3}\rangle~,\quad\mathbb{A}_{+,\text{II}}=\langle a_{2}a_{3}\rangle~,

(5.50)

where
\mathbb{A}_{-}
has two choices. For the choice of
\mathbb{A}_{-,\text{I}}
, the interface
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
is the identity interface in the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]
together with a decoupled 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
gauge theory. And for the choice of
\mathbb{A}_{-,\text{II}}
,
\mathcal{I}_{-+,\text{II}}
is simply the identity interface in the minimal TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{7,-2}[a^{(2)}]
. Hence, we find

\mathbf{\left[F_{D_{3},{D}_{3},\overline{D}_{3}}^{D_{3}}\right]_{SU(7)_{-1}\overline{D}_{3},\mathbb{1}}}=\text{decoupled $\mathbb{Z}_{7}$ gauge theory \quad or \quad trivial TQFT}~.

(5.51)

Appendix A
Fusion junctions and associators in the 2d compact boson Theory

A.1
Local Fusion Junctions

In this subsection, we analyze the
T\times T\to\eta^{k}
junction using the junction data for the fusion
T\times\eta^{-k}\to T
, and then complete our analysis of the local fusion junctions by analyzing the
\eta^{k}\times T=T
and
\eta^{m}\times\eta^{n}=\eta^{m+n}
junctions.

T\times T\to\eta^{k}
using
T\times\eta^{-k}\to T

T\times\eta^{-k}\to T
and flip the direction of the
\eta^{k}
defect along
L_{2}
and similarly also that of the
T

t>0
, as described in Figure

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\phi_{3}\mathrm{d}\phi_{1}={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{0}_{\infty}\phi_{1}\mathrm{d}\phi_{3}-\left.{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{1}\phi_{3}\right|^{0}_{\infty}=\hbox to51.25pt{\vbox to45.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.78162pt\lower-27.93683pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(A.2)

Combining the results from
T\times\eta^{-k}=T
(
3.18
) with a suitable relabeling of the fields and the extra boundary term (
A.2
) needed for flipping the direction of
T
, one reproduces the boundary data (
3.29
).

\eta^{k}\times T=T

Consider fusing the upper part of the two defects
\eta^{k}
and
T
to obtain the local fusion junction in Figure
15
(left).

\eta^{k},\varphi
L_{1}~
T
~L_{2}
T
\phi_{1}
\phi_{2}
\phi_{3}
0
\eta^{m},\varphi_{1}
L_{1}~
\eta^{n},\varphi_{2}
~L_{2}
\eta^{m+n},\varphi
\phi_{1}
\phi_{2}
\phi_{3}
0

Figure 15
:
The local fusion junctions Hom(
\eta^{k}\times T,T
) (left) and Hom(
\eta^{m}\times\eta^{n},\eta^{m+n}
) (right).

The defect action in the fused part at
t>0
is given by

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle i\over 2\pi}\int_{L_{1}}\mathrm{d}\varphi\left(\phi_{1}-\phi_{3}+{2\pi k\over N}\right)+{iN\over 2\pi}\int_{L_{2}}\phi_{3}\mathrm{d}\phi_{2}

(A.3)

\displaystyle+{i\over 2\pi}\int_{t>0}\left[N\phi_{1}\mathrm{d}\phi_{2}+\left(\phi_{1}-\phi_{3}+{2\pi k\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}\left(\varphi-N\phi_{2}\right)\right]+\left.ik\phi_{2}\right|^{\infty}_{0}

where the first term on the second line corresponds to the
T
-duality defect as expected from the fusion outcome, the second term is a trivial decoupled TQFT and the last term is a boundary term. The trivial TQFT in the current form is such that its Dirichlet boundary conditions are consistent with the relation between the bulk fields across the
\eta^{k}
defect,
\left.\phi_{1}-\phi_{3}+{2\pi k\over N}\right|_{L_{1}}=0
mod
2\pi
. The Dirichlet boundary conditions and the boundary term at this junction can be summarized as

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(\eta^{k}\times T,T):

\displaystyle\left.\phi_{1}-\phi_{3}+{2\pi k\over N}\right|_{t=0}=0\,,\quad\left.\varphi-N\phi_{2}\right|_{t=0}=0\quad\text{mod $2\pi$}

(A.4)

\displaystyle S_{bdry}=-ik\phi_{2}(0)\,.

\eta^{m}\times\eta^{n}=\eta^{m+n}

Following the same steps for the local fusion junction
\eta^{m}\times\eta^{n}=\eta^{m+n}
in Figure
15
(right), the action along the fused segment takes the form

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle i\over 2\pi}\int_{t>0}\left[\mathrm{d}\varphi_{1}\left(\phi_{1}-\phi_{3}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)+\mathrm{d}\varphi_{2}\left(\phi_{3}-\phi_{2}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)\right]

(A.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle i\over 2\pi}\int_{t>0}\left[\mathrm{d}\varphi_{1}\left(\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}\right)+\left(\phi_{3}-\phi_{2}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\right]\,.

The first term on the second line is the action for the expected
\eta^{m+n}
defect and the second term is a trivial decoupled TQFT, whose Dirichlet boundary condition is consistent with the relation between the bulk fields on the defect. Hence, there is no boundary term but the boundary condition is given by

\displaystyle\text{Hom}(\eta^{m}\times\eta^{n},\eta^{m+n}):

\displaystyle\left.\phi_{3}-\phi_{2}+{2\pi n\over N}\right|_{t=0}=0\,,\quad\left.\varphi\right|_{t=0}=\left.\varphi_{2}\right|_{t=0}=\left.\varphi_{1}\right|_{t=0}\quad\text{mod $2\pi$}

(A.6)

\displaystyle S_{bdry}=0\,.

A.2
F
-symbols

In this subsection, we compute all the
F
-symbols in the 2d compact boson theory except for
F^{T}_{T,T,T}
. In Figure
16
, we collected the diagrams for the first three
F
-symbols.

Figure 16
:
The diagrams for
F^{\eta^{m}}_{T,\eta^{n},T},F^{T}_{\eta^{m},T,\eta^{n}}
, and
F^{T}_{\eta^{m},\eta^{n},T}
.

F^{\eta^{m}}_{T,\eta^{n},T}

Using the results from the previous subsection, the boundary conditions and boundary terms at each junction are

\displaystyle\alpha:

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I}=0\,,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}=0\,,\quad S(\alpha)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}\left(\phi_{R}-{2\pi m\over N}\right),

(A.7)

\displaystyle\beta:

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}=0\,,\quad\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{R}=0\,,\quad S(\beta)=-in\phi_{R}(\beta),

\displaystyle\gamma:

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}=0\,,\quad\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{L}=0\,,\quad S(\gamma)={iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{L}\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}\right),

\displaystyle\delta:

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I}^{\prime}=0\,,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}=0\,,\quad S(\delta)={iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}^{\prime}\left(\phi_{R}+{2\pi\eta_{2}\over N}\right).

The result of the global fusion of the three defects,
T,\eta^{n},T
in the middle is

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\mathrm{d}\phi_{I}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}\left(\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{R}\right)

(A.8)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\left[-im\phi_{I}+{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{R}\phi_{I}+in\phi_{R}\right]^{\beta}_{\gamma}}_{S_{bdry;global}}

where the first term is the expected
\eta^{m}
defect, the second term is a trivial decoupled TQFT, and the last term is a collection of boundary terms. Both decoupled TQFT fields
\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{R}
and
\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}
have trivial Dirichlet boundary conditions from the boundary conditions (
A.7
), and hence the decoupled TQFT does not contribute to the
F
-symbol after reducing the interval. Adding the boundary terms at the upper junction,

S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\beta}=0

(A.9)

and at the lower junction,

\displaystyle S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\delta}

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)-{2\pi imn\over N}

(A.10)

\displaystyle=-{2\pi imn\over N}\quad\text{mod $2\pi$}\,.

Hence, the
F
-symbol is

F^{\eta^{m}}_{T,\eta^{n},T}=\exp\left({{2\pi imn\over N}}\right)\,.

(A.11)

F^{T}_{\eta^{m},T,\eta^{n}}

Using the results from the previous subsection, the boundary conditions (modulo
2\pi
) and the boundary terms at each junction are

\displaystyle\alpha:

\displaystyle\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{R}=0,\quad S(\alpha)=-im\phi_{R}(\alpha),

(A.12)

\displaystyle\beta:

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I}=0,\quad\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad S(\beta)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right),

\displaystyle\gamma:

\displaystyle\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{I}^{\prime}=0,\quad S(\gamma)=im\phi_{I}^{\prime}(\gamma),

\displaystyle\delta:

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I}=0,\quad\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad S(\delta)={iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{L}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)

and the result of the global fusion of
\eta^{m},T,\eta^{n}
in the middle is

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\phi_{L}\mathrm{d}\phi_{R}+\left[{i\over 2\pi}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)\varphi_{2}+im\phi_{I}^{\prime}\right]^{\beta}_{\gamma}

(A.13)

\displaystyle+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}(\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{I}^{\prime})-{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{L}\right)\mathrm{d}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right).

The trivial TQFT fields have trivial Dirichlet boundary conditions and do not contribute to the
F
-symbol. Adding the boundary terms at the upper junction,

\displaystyle S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\beta}

\displaystyle={i\over 2\pi}(\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I})\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)+im\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}\right)

(A.14)

\displaystyle=-{2\pi imn\over N}

and at the lower junction

\displaystyle S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\delta}

\displaystyle={i\over 2\pi}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)(N\phi_{L}-\varphi_{2})=0\,.

(A.15)

Hence,

F_{\eta^{m},T,\eta^{n}}^{T}=\exp\left({2\pi imn\over N}\right)~.

(A.16)

F^{T}_{\eta^{m},\eta^{n},T}

The boundary conditions and boundary terms at each junction are

\displaystyle\alpha:

\displaystyle\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{R}=0,\quad S(\alpha)=-im\phi_{R}(\alpha)~,

(A.17)

\displaystyle\beta:

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{R}=0,\quad S(\beta)=-in\phi_{R}(\beta)~,

\displaystyle\delta:

\displaystyle\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}=0,\quad\varphi-N\phi_{R}=0,\quad S(\delta)=i(m+n)\phi_{R}(\delta)~,

\displaystyle\gamma:

\displaystyle\varphi=\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2},\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad S(\gamma)=0~.

The global fusion of
\eta^{m},\eta^{n},T
in the middle is

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\phi_{L}\mathrm{d}\phi_{R}+\left.i(m+n)\phi_{R}\right|^{\beta}_{\gamma}

(A.18)

\displaystyle+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}(\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2})+\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}(\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{R})~,

and the trivial decoupled TQFT fields have the trivial Dirichlet boundary conditions. The sums of the boundary terms at the upper and lower junctions vanish, and thus the
F
-symbol is trivial,

F^{T}_{\eta^{m},\eta^{n},T}=1\,.

(A.19)

Figure 17
:
The diagrams for
