and
F^{\eta^{m}}_{\eta^{n},T,T}
.

F^{T}_{T,\eta^{m},\eta^{n}}

The boundary conditions (mod
2\pi
) and boundary terms at each junction are

\displaystyle\alpha:

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}=0,\quad\varphi-N\phi_{L}=0,\quad S(\alpha)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{L}\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}\right)~,

(A.20)

\displaystyle\beta:

\displaystyle\varphi=\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2},\quad\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad S(\beta)=0~,

\displaystyle\gamma:

\displaystyle\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{L}=0,\quad S(\gamma)={iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{L}\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)~,

\displaystyle\delta:

\displaystyle\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{L}=0,\quad S(\delta)={iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{L}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)~,

and the global fusion of the three defects
\eta^{m},\eta^{n},T
is

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\phi_{L}\mathrm{d}\phi_{R}+\left[{i\over 2\pi}\varphi_{1}\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}\right)\right]^{\beta}_{\gamma}

(A.21)

\displaystyle+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left[(N\phi_{L}-\varphi_{1})\mathrm{d}(\phi_{I}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N})+\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}-{2\pi\eta_{2}\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\right]\,.

All trivial decoupled TQFT fields have the trivial Dirichlet boundary conditions and so the two decoupled TQFTs do not contribute to the
F
-symbol. The sum of the boundary terms at the upper junction is given by

S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\beta}={i\over 2\pi}(\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{L})\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}\right)=0\quad\text{mod $2\pi$}

(A.22)

and at the lower junction by

S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\gamma}={i\over 2\pi}(N\phi_{L}-\varphi_{1})\left(\phi_{I}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi(m+n)\over N}\right)=0\quad\text{mod $2\pi$}\,.

(A.23)

Hence, the
F
-symbol is trivial,

F^{T}_{T,\eta^{m},\eta^{n}}=1\,.

(A.24)

F^{\eta^{m}}_{T,T,\eta^{n}}

The boundary conditions (mod
2\pi
) and boundary terms at each junction are

\displaystyle\alpha:

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{I}=0,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad S(\alpha)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}\left(\phi_{R}-{2\pi m\over N}\right),

(A.25)

\displaystyle\beta:

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I}=0,\quad\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad S(\beta)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}\right),

\displaystyle\gamma:

\displaystyle\varphi-N\phi_{I}=0,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}^{\prime}+{2\pi(m-n)\over N}=0,\quad S(\gamma)={iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-{2\pi(m-n)\over N}\right),

\displaystyle\delta:

\displaystyle\varphi=\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2},\quad\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad S(\delta)=0~,

and the global fusion of the three defects
T,T,\eta^{n}
in the middle is

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}\phi_{I}+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi n\over N})\mathrm{d}(\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I})

(A.26)

\displaystyle+{iN\over 2\pi}\left[\phi_{I}\left(\phi_{I}^{\prime}-{2\pi(m-n)\over N}\right)\right]^{\beta}_{\gamma}\,.

The trivial decoupled TQFT fields have trivial Dirichlet boundary conditions. The sum of the boundary terms at the upper junction vanishes,

S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\beta}=0~,

(A.27)

and the sum at the lower junction vanishes as well,

S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\gamma}=0\,.

(A.28)

Hence, the
F
-symbol is trivial:

F^{\eta^{m}}_{T,T,\eta^{n}}=1\,.

(A.29)

F^{\eta^{m}}_{\eta^{n},T,T}

The boundary conditions (mod
2\pi
) and boundary terms at each junction are

\displaystyle\alpha:

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}=\varphi,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad S(\alpha)=0,

(A.30)

\displaystyle\beta:

\displaystyle\varphi-N\phi_{I}^{\prime}=0,\quad\phi_{I}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi(m-n)\over N}=0,\quad S(\beta)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}^{\prime}\left(\phi_{R}-{2\pi(m-n)\over N}\right),

\displaystyle\gamma:

\displaystyle\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi n\over N}=0,\quad\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I}^{\prime}=0,\quad S(\gamma)=in\phi_{I}^{\prime}(\gamma),

\displaystyle\delta:

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}-N\phi_{I}^{\prime}=0,\quad\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}=0,\quad S(\delta)={iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}^{\prime}\left(\phi_{R}-{2\pi m\over N}\right)

and the global fusion of the three defects
\eta^{n},T,T
in the middle is

\displaystyle S=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\mathrm{d}\varphi_{1}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)+\left[{iN\over 2\pi}\phi_{I}^{\prime}\left(\phi_{R}-{2\pi(m-n)\over N}\right)\right]^{\beta}_{\gamma}

(A.31)

\displaystyle+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{R}+{2\pi m\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}(N\phi_{I}^{\prime}-\varphi_{1})+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(\phi_{L}-\phi_{I}+{2\pi n\over N}\right)\mathrm{d}(\varphi_{2}-N\phi_{I}^{\prime})\,.

All decoupled trivial TQFT fields have trivial Dirichlet boundary conditions, and the sum of the boundary terms at both the upper and lower junctions vanishes,

S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\beta}=0\,,\quad S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\gamma}=0\,.

(A.32)

Hence, the
F
-symbol is trivial,

F^{\eta^{m}}_{\eta^{n},T,T}=1\,.

(A.33)

Appendix B
Comments on alternative formalisms on the local junctions

The topological boundary/interface of a topological defect (or a TFT) can be described either in terms of boundary conditions or by introducing boundary degrees of freedom. In Section
4
, we take the first approach, and in this Appendix, we want to present the alternative description commonly used in the literature
[
13
,
20
]
and discuss their relations in the concrete example of the 3d
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory written in terms of the CS action. Notice that there are no new results here, and we simply hope to clarify a few subtleties in various formalisms.

Let’s first recall the topological boundary condition for
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory given by the CS action

S=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{V}a\wedge db~.

(B.1)

For generic
N>1
, there always exist two boundary conditions

\displaystyle\text{(I)}

\displaystyle:\quad a|_{\partial V}=d\phi_{a}~,\quad Nb|_{\partial V}=d\phi_{b}~,

(B.2)

\displaystyle\text{(II)}

\displaystyle:Na|_{\partial V}=d\phi_{a}~,\quad\quad b|_{\partial V}=d\phi_{b}~,

and we will restrict our discussion to these two for simplicity.

In the boundary condition (I), the gauge transformations are given by

a\rightarrow a+d\lambda_{a}~,\quad b\rightarrow b+d\lambda_{b}~,\quad\phi_{a}\rightarrow\phi_{a}+\lambda_{a}~,\quad\phi_{b}\rightarrow\phi_{b}+N\lambda_{b}~.

(B.3)

It is straightforward to check that the action is gauge invariant and is consistent with the variation principle. In this boundary condition, the
a
-Wilson line is allowed to terminate on the boundary, described by the gauge-invariant operator

\exp\left(-i\phi_{a}(p)+i\int_{\gamma}a\right)~,\quad\gamma\cap\partial V=\{p\}~,

(B.4)

where
\gamma
is some path in the bulk
V
that terminates at the boundary point
p\in\partial V
. On the boundary, the charge
N

b
-Wilson line is identified with the identity line as there exists a local junction between the two. In other words, the line
e^{iN\int b}
can terminate on the vertex operator
e^{-i\phi_{b}}
in a gauge-invariant way
31
31

31

Notice that similar story happens in the bulk, except the role of
e^{i\phi_{b}}
is played by the monopole operator of
a

[
29
]
.
. Hence,
e^{i\oint_{\gamma}b}
generates
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry on the boundary for the boundary condition (I). Combined, we see the boundary condition (I) yields the correct operator spectrum for the
e^{i\oint a}
-condensed boundary. It is straightforward to repeat the same analysis for the boundary condition (II) and see it describes the
e^{i\oint b}
-condensed boundary correctly.

The same boundary conditions can also be described by introducing boundary scalars and coupling them to the bulk field, while leaving the bulk fields completely free in the action on the boundary. The boundary coupling as well as the gauge transformations are given by

\displaystyle\text{(I)}

\displaystyle:S_{\partial V}=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{\partial V}\phi db~,

(B.5)

\displaystyle a\rightarrow a+d\lambda_{a}~,\quad b\rightarrow b+d\lambda_{b}~,\quad\phi\rightarrow\phi-\lambda_{a}~,

\displaystyle\text{(II)}

\displaystyle:S_{\partial V}=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{\partial V}\phi da+a\wedge b~,

\displaystyle a\rightarrow a+d\lambda_{a}~,\quad b\rightarrow b+d\lambda_{b}~,\quad\phi\rightarrow\phi-\lambda_{b}~.

It is straightforward to check that both boundary conditions are well-defined and yield the correct operator spectrum. In the boundary condition (I),
e^{i\int a}
can terminate on the
e^{-i\phi}
in a gauge-invariant way on the boundary. At the same time, the equation of motion of
\phi
implies that
e^{iN\oint b}=\mathbb{1}
thus
e^{i\oint b}
generates
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
symmetry on the boundary
32
32

32

To see that
e^{iN\int b}
can terminate on the boundary, we notice that there exists a boundary operator
\mathcal{O}(x)
, defined by removing the point
x
from
\partial V
and require the compact scalar
\phi
to have unit winding around
x
when inserting in the path integral.
e^{iN\int b}
can terminate gauge-invariantly on
\mathcal{O}(x)
.
. Similarly, in the boundary condition (II),
e^{i\int b}
can terminate on the boundary while
e^{i\oint a}
generates
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-symmetry instead.

The advantage of this formalism is that one can manifestly produce an action in terms of continuous fields in the interval reduction. For instance, let’s consider placing the theory on
[0,1]_{t}\times M_{2}
. Let us first consider choosing boundary condition (I) for both
t=0
and
t=1
boundaries. In this case, the full action is given by

\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{[0,1]_{t}\times M_{2}}a\wedge db-\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{M_{2}|_{t=0}}\phi\,db+\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{M_{2}|_{t=1}}\widetilde{\phi}\,db~,

(B.6)

with the gauge transformation

a\rightarrow a+d\lambda_{a}~,\quad b\rightarrow b+d\lambda_{b}~,\quad\phi\rightarrow\phi-\lambda_{a}~,\quad\widetilde{\phi}\rightarrow\phi-\lambda_{a}~.

(B.7)

Reducing along the interval by fusing two boundaries leads to the 2d action

\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{M_{2}}(\widetilde{\phi}-\phi)\,db\equiv\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{M_{2}}\Phi\,db~,

(B.8)

which becomes the standard 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory upon introducing the gauge invariant compact scalar
\Phi=\widetilde{\phi}-\phi
, thus reproduces the well-known result.

On the other hand, one can also consider choosing boundary condition (I) for the
t=0
boundary and boundary condition (II) for the
t=1
boundary. In this case, the full action is given by

\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{[0,1]_{t}\times M_{2}}a\wedge db-\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{M_{2}|_{t=0}}(\phi\,da+a\wedge b)+\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{M_{2}|_{t=1}}\widetilde{\phi}\,db~,

(B.9)

with the gauge transformation

a\rightarrow a+d\lambda_{a}~,\quad b\rightarrow b+d\lambda_{b}~,\quad\phi\rightarrow\phi-\lambda_{b}~,\quad\widetilde{\phi}\rightarrow\phi-\lambda_{a}~.

(B.10)

Upon the reduction, we find the 2d action

\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{M_{2}}-a\wedge b-\phi\,da+\widetilde{\phi}\,db~,

(B.11)

with the gauge transformation (
B.10
). Despite having a seemingly non-trivial action, the 2d theory is actually trivial as there are no gauge-invariant operators which do not commute upon canonical quantization, also matching the well-known result.

Appendix C
Triality fusion junctions and associators in 4d Maxwell theory

C.1
Triality fusion junctions

The triality and the condensation defects form a closed non-invertible fusion algebra. In this section, we focus on the associator for the fusion of three triality defects (not of a condensation defect) for even

and analyze the following relevant local fusion and morphism junctions,

D_{3}\times\mathbb{1}=D_{3}~\eqref{eq: bo},\quad\mathbb{1}\times D_{3}=D_{3}~\eqref{eq: bk},\quad\mathbb{1}\times\overline{D}_{3}=\overline{D}_{3}~\eqref{eq: wb},\quad\overline{D}_{3}\times\mathbb{1}=\overline{D}_{3}~\eqref{eq: wc}\\
D_{3}\times D_{3}=U(1)_{N}\overline{D}_{3}~\eqref{eq: bf},\quad\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=U(1)_{-N}D_{3}~\eqref{eq: bff},\\
\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}=C_{0}~\eqref{eq: wd},\quad D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=C_{N\over 2}~\eqref{eq: we}\,.\end{gathered}

C.2.1

This associator (first diagram of Figure

) relates the following two different fusions:

\displaystyle(D_{3}\times D_{3})\times D_{3}

\displaystyle=U(1)_{N}\,\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}=U(1)_{N}\,C_{0}\to U(1)_{N}~,

\displaystyle=U(1)_{N}\,D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}=U(1)_{N}\,C_{N\over 2}\to U(1)_{N}~.

Using the junction analyses in the previous subsection, the boundary data at each junction is

\displaystyle\alpha\beta

\displaystyle:\quad A_{L}-A_{R},\quad N(A_{I}+A_{R})+{N\over 2}(A_{L}-A_{R})-b=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}

\displaystyle S(\alpha)+S(\beta)={iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha\beta}}\left[A_{R}\wedge A_{I}-A_{L}\wedge A_{R}+(A_{I}-A_{R})\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}\right]~,

\displaystyle\gamma\delta:

\displaystyle A_{L}-A_{R},\quad b+NA_{I}^{\prime}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}~,

\displaystyle S(\gamma)+S(\delta)=-{iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\gamma\delta}}\left[A_{L}\wedge A_{I}+A_{L}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}-2A_{R}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}\right]~.

After the parallel fuison of three
D_{3}
defects in the middle,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+{1\over 2}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{L}+A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}+{1\over 2}A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+{1\over 2}A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle={iN\over 4\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})\quad\leftarrow U(1)_{N}

\displaystyle-{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}\,(A_{L}-A_{R})\quad\leftarrow\text{ Condensation defect }{C}_{0}

\displaystyle+{iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}-\Sigma_{\gamma}}\left[-A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{R}+{1\over 2}\left(A_{I}\wedge A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{L}+A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{I}\right)\right]\quad\leftarrow S_{bdry;global}

the interval hosts a

Chern-Simons theory, a condensation defect and some boundary terms. Summing the boundary terms from the upper junctions along with
S_{bdry;global}
we get

S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{L})\wedge(A_{R}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})~,

(C.18)

while the sum of boundary terms at the lower junctions vanishes,

\displaystyle S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\gamma\delta}=0~.

(C.19)

The total boundary term (
C.18
) at the upper junction connects the
U(1)_{N}
theories at the junction
\beta
. To see this, we notice that the
U(1)_{N}
theories above
\beta
, below
\gamma
and between them are

\displaystyle\text{Above $\beta$}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle iN\over 4\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\beta}(A_{R}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}(A_{R}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})~,

(C.20)

\displaystyle[\gamma,\beta]\text{ and }\text{Below $\gamma$}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle iN\over 4\pi}\int^{\beta}_{-\infty}(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})~.

Following a similar discussion as in Section
4.2.3
(or applying the folding trick here), we see that the boundary term (
C.18
) ensures the gauge invariance from both sides, and the gluing condition,
(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})-(A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}
, indicates that this is a trivial interface of the
U(1)_{N}
theory.

Thus, we are left with a 3d condensation defect on the interval. Reducing it along the interval leads to a 2d condensation defect of the bulk 1-form symmetry (which is simply a projector). Notice that this is consistent with the operator spectrum analysis. First, as follows from the boundary conditions, the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
higher quantum symmetry line
e^{i\int(A_{L}-A_{R})}
on the 3d condensation defect can stretch between the two boundaries. After reduction, it becomes a topological local operator and can be interpreted as the higher quantum 1-form symmetry on the 2d condensation defect. Second, in the 4d bulk, an open
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry operator can be viewed as a non-genuine line operator bounding the symmetry operator; but on the condensation defect,
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
is gauged and this line operator should become genuine. This is guaranteed by the e.o.m. of
A_{L}
(or
A_{R}
), which allows us to rewrite such an open
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry operator as the
A_{I}^{\prime}
-Wilson line
e^{i\oint A_{I}^{\prime}}
. After reduction,
e^{i\oint A_{I}^{\prime}}
remains a line operator by the boundary conditions, encoding the fact that an open
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry operator becomes genuine in the 2d condensation defect.

We also want to highlight that the existence of local junctions
C_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{1}
in the initial diamond diagrams we considered does not necessarily imply there will be a reduction of the condensation defect in the end. This is because we will need to perform the global fusion to collapse the diagram, during which the condensation defect can become a decoupled
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-gauge theory. Only when the condensation defect appears in the global fusion, will we have the interval reduction of the 3d condensation defect which leads to the 2d condensation defect.

Overall, we see that the 2d theory corresponding to the associator 1-morphism results solely from the 3d condensation defect in the interval, which reduces to a 2d condensation defect. As a result,

\left[F^{U(1)_{N}}_{D_{3},D_{3},D_{3}}\right]_{U(1)_{N}\overline{D}_{3},U(1)_{N}\overline{D}_{3}}=\text{2d $\mathbb{Z}_{N}$ condensation defect}\,.

(C.21)

C.2.2
\left[F^{D_{3}}_{D_{3},\overline{D}_{3},D_{3}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}

This associator relates the following fusions above and below as depicted in the second diagram of Figure
19
:

\displaystyle D_{3}\times(\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3})

\displaystyle=D_{3}\times C_{0}\to D_{3}\times\mathbb{1}=D_{3}~,

(C.22)

\displaystyle(D_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3})\times D_{3}

\displaystyle=C_{N\over 2}\times D_{3}\to\mathbb{1}\times D_{3}=D_{3}~.

The boundary data at each junction can be collected to

\displaystyle\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}:

\displaystyle b+NA_{L},\quad b+NA_{I}^{\prime},\quad A_{I}-A_{R}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}~,

(C.23)

\displaystyle S(\alpha)=-{i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}b\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R}),\quad S(\beta)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}}A_{R}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}~,

\displaystyle\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\delta:

\displaystyle N(A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime})-b^{\prime},\quad A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime},\quad b-NA_{L}-NA_{R}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}~,

\displaystyle S(\gamma)=-{iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\gamma}}\left(A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{I}-{1\over 2}A_{L}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}\right),\quad S(\delta)=-{iN\over 4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\delta}}A_{L}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}~.

Fusing the three defects in the middle by summing their actions result in the following actions:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+{1\over 2}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{L}-A_{I}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}-{1\over 2}A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+{1\over 2}A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{I}^{\prime}\right)

(C.24)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+{1\over 2}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{L}\right)}_{D_{3}}+\underbrace{{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}(A_{I}-A_{R})}_{\text{decoupled $\mathbb{Z}_{N}$ gauge theory}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{{iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}-\Sigma_{\gamma}}A_{I}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}}_{S_{bdry;global}}~.

Collecting the boundary terms, at the upper junctions

\displaystyle S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-{i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha\beta}}(b+NA_{I}^{\prime})\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})\in 2\pi i\mathbb{Z}

(C.25)

where we used the boundary conditions, and at the lower junctions,

\displaystyle S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle=0~.

(C.26)

Hence, we find a decoupled 3d
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
on the interval. Since
A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}
has
(ND)
and
A_{I}-A_{R}
has
(DN)
boundary conditions on
\alpha\beta
and
\gamma\delta
respectively, there is no untwisted local operators after reduction and the decoupled
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory becomes the trivial theory after reducing the interval
[\gamma,\beta]
.
Hence,

\left[F^{D_{3}}_{D_{3},\overline{D}_{3},D_{3}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}=\text{Trivial theory}\,.

(C.27)

C.2.3
[F^{D_{3}}_{\overline{D}_{3},D_{3},D_{3}}]_{\mathbb{1},U(1)_{N}\overline{D}_{3}}

Lastly, let’s compute the associator in the last diagram of
19
which relates

\displaystyle\text{Upper part}:

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{3}\times(D_{3}\times D_{3})=\overline{D}_{3}\times\overline{D}_{3}~U(1)_{N}=D_{3}~U(1)_{-N}\times U(1)_{N}\to D_{3}~,

(C.28)

\displaystyle\text{Lower part}:

\displaystyle(\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3})\times D_{3}=C_{0}\times D_{3}\to\mathbb{1}\times D_{3}=D_{3}~.

The boundary data at each junction is

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime},\alpha,\beta:

\displaystyle a_{1}=A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R}~,\quad a_{2}=a_{L}+A_{I}+A_{R}~,

(C.29)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(1)&N(A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I}+A_{R}),\quad A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}~,\\
\text{or }(2)&A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}+2(A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{I}+A_{R}),\quad{N\over 2}(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}~,\end{cases}

\displaystyle S(\alpha)=-\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}A_{L}\wedge A_{R}+(A_{L}-A_{R})\wedge A_{I}~,S(\beta)=\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}}A_{I}\wedge A_{R}+(A_{I}-A_{R})\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}~,

\displaystyle S(\alpha^{\prime})=-\frac{iN}{4\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}}(A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}+A_{R})\wedge(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{R})~,

\displaystyle\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\delta:

\displaystyle A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime},\quad N(A_{L}+A_{I}+A_{R})=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}~,

\displaystyle S(\gamma)=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge A_{I}~,\quad S(\delta)=-\frac{iN}{2\pi}A_{L}\wedge A_{I}^{\prime}~.

Next, we fuse the three defects
\overline{D}_{3}\times D_{3}\times D_{3}
in the middle by summing their actions,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{\gamma}^{\beta}(-A_{L}dA_{I}-\frac{1}{2}A_{I}dA_{I}+A_{I}dA_{I}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}A_{I}dA_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}dA_{R}+\frac{1}{2}A_{I}^{\prime}dA_{I}^{\prime})

(C.30)

\displaystyle=\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left(A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{R}+\frac{1}{2}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{L}\right)\quad(\leftarrow\,D_{3})

\displaystyle+\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}\left[\frac{1}{2}(A_{I}^{\prime}-A_{L})\mathrm{d}(A_{I}^{\prime}-A_{L})+(A_{I}^{\prime}-A_{L})\wedge\mathrm{d}(A_{I}+A_{L}+A_{R})\right]\quad(\leftarrow(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N})

\displaystyle+\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}-\Sigma_{\gamma}}\left[A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge(A_{I}{+}\frac{1}{2}A_{L})\right]\quad(\leftarrow\,S_{bdry;global})~,

where the first line is the action for
D_{3}
as expected from the fusion algebra, the second line is a decoupled
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory with the twist
N
(denoted by
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
and the last term is the boundary term.

The total boundary term at the upper junction is

\displaystyle S(\alpha)+S(\beta)+S(\alpha^{\prime})+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\beta}

\displaystyle=0~,

(C.31)

and the total boundary term at the lower junction is

S(\gamma)+S(\delta)+\left.S_{bdry;global}\right|_{\gamma}=0~.

(C.32)

Therefore, we indeed find a decoupled
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}
theory on the interval.

On the upper junction, we have two boundary conditions (1) and (2) from condensing
U(1)_{N}\times U(1)_{-N}
. Under the boundary condition (1), the upper and lower junctions have the same boundary conditions on gauge fields
A_{I}+A_{L}+A_{R}
and
A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}
as written in Table
9
. Following the discussion in Section
4.3.1
, reducing the interval results in a 2d
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory.

A_{I}+A_{L}+A_{R}

A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}

\alpha\beta\,(1)

\mathbb{Z}_{N}

pure
U(1)
gauge

\gamma\gamma^{\prime}

\mathbb{Z}_{N}

pure
U(1)
gauge

Table 9
:
Boundary conditions for the case (1) of Eq. (
C.29
)

Under the boundary condition (2), on the other hand, the gauge fields have different boundary conditions at the upper and lower junctions, as written in Table
10
. In this case, we only find
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
untwisted local operators arising from the Wilson lines
e^{i\frac{Nk}{2}\oint a_{1}+a_{2}}
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
). Hence, reducing the interval
[\gamma,\beta]
results in a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gauge theory.

(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})+2(A_{I}+A_{L}+A_{R})

A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}

\alpha\beta\,(2)

pure gauge

\mathbb{Z}_{N\over 2}

\gamma\gamma^{\prime}

\mathbb{Z}_{N\over 2}

pure gauge

Table 10
:
Boundary conditions for the case (2) of Eq. (
C.29
)

In summary,

(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{N}\quad\overset{\text{reducing interval}}{\longrightarrow}\quad\begin{cases}(1):&\mathbb{Z}_{N}\text{ gauge theory}~,\\
(2):&\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ gauge theory}~.\end{cases}

(C.33)

Combining the reduction of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
theory on the interval and the boundary actions, there are two choices for the associator 1-morphism,

\left[F^{D_{3}}_{\overline{D}_{3},D_{3},D_{3}}\right]_{U(1)_{N}\overline{D}_{3},\mathbb{1}}=\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{N}$ gauge theory}\quad\text{or}\quad\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ gauge theory}~.

(C.34)

(D.2)

From the math literature of anyon condensation
[
21
,
30
]
, the interface
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
is given the fusion category of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
-modules in
\mathcal{T}
33
33

33

Here we view
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
as an algebra object in
\mathcal{T}
.
, and the theory
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
on the other side of the interface is described by the category of local
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
-modules (which is a modular tensor category). The interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
carry the structure as bimodule categories of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
. Physically, condensing
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
means gauging the 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
. The 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
can act on the line operators by fusion and by braiding, and after gauging we keep only the operators invariant under
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
action. On the interface
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
,
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
can only act by fusion as
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
is 2-dimension. The simple invariant line operators on
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
must be invariant under fusion with any lines in
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
, therefore must take the form

\ell_{b}^{\pm}\equiv\sum_{a\in\mathbb{A}_{\pm}}b\times a

(D.3)

for any simple line operator
b
in
\mathcal{T}
. Clearly,
\ell_{b}^{\pm}
and
\ell_{b^{\prime}}^{\pm}
parameterize the same invariant lines if
b^{-1}b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
. The
\ell_{b}^{\pm}
constructed above is a
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
-module, and all the
\ell_{b}^{\pm}
’s form the category
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{A}_{\pm}}
of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
-modules describing the topological line operators on the interface
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
. The fusion rules for those invariant lines are simply given by
34
34

34

Notice that the tensor product of
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
-modules is the balanced tensor product
\otimes_{\mathbb{A}_{\pm}}
over the algebra object
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
which gives rise to the correct multiplicity below.

\ell_{b}^{\pm}\times\ell_{b^{\prime}}^{\pm}=\ell_{bb^{\prime}}^{\pm}~.

(D.4)

It is also possible to fuse the bulk line in
\mathcal{T}
with the interface lines with the fusion rules

\ell_{b}^{+}\otimes b^{\prime}=\ell^{+}_{bb^{\prime}}~,\quad b^{\prime}\otimes\ell_{b}^{-}=\ell_{b^{\prime}b}^{-}~,\quad\forall b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}~.

(D.5)

On the other hand, in the theory
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
acquired by gauging
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
, where the 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
also acts via braiding. Hence, only the
\ell_{b}
’s that braid trivially with
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
are invariant under the
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
action
35
35

35

For that, we only need to check any one of the line in the sum braids trivially with all the lines in
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
. This is because in order to condense
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
, every anyon in
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
must be boson and braids trivially with each other.
. Those
\ell_{b}
’s are known as local
\mathbb{A}_{\pm}
-modules, and together they form a modular tensor category (often denoted as
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{A}_{\pm}}^{loc}
) describing the 3d TQFT
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
.

Next, we explain the domain wall fusion step between
\mathcal{I}_{+}
and
\mathcal{I}_{-}
in (
D.1
). The outcome is a domain wall
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
between
\mathcal{T}_{-}
and
\mathcal{T}_{+}
, which we identify as the
F
-symbol. The result has been summarized in Table
7
, and the result is derived from the math tools known as balanced Deligne tensor product which captures the domain wall fusion as shown in
[
30
]

\mathcal{I}_{+-}=\mathcal{I}_{+}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{I_{-}}~.

(D.6)

In the special case where
\mathcal{T}
contains only Abelian anyons, the computation can be done as follows
36
36

36

For experts, here we are simply using the result that, for a fusion category
\mathcal{C}
, the balanced tensor product
\mathcal{M}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{N}
between a right
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{M}
and a left
\mathcal{C}
-module category
\mathcal{N}
can be computed by first viewing
\mathcal{M}\boxtimes\mathcal{N}
as a module category over
\mathcal{C}\boxtimes\mathcal{C}^{rev}
and taking the category of modules over the algebra
\mathcal{A}=\oplus_{X\in Irr(\mathcal{C})}X\boxtimes X^{*}
in
\mathcal{M}\boxtimes\mathcal{N}
. For more details, see Remark 3.9 in
[
31
]
.
. First, one takes
\mathcal{I}_{+}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{-}
corresponding to naively stacking two interfaces on top of each other. Using our previous notation, simple line operators on
\mathcal{I}_{+}\boxtimes\mathcal{I}_{-}
can be labeled as

\ell_{b,b^{\prime}}\equiv\ell_{b}^{+}\boxtimes\ell_{b}^{-}~.

(D.7)

To get the spectrum of line operators in
\mathcal{I}_{+-}=\mathcal{I}_{+}\boxtimes_{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{I}_{-}
, one can simply make the identification

\ell_{b,b^{\prime}}\simeq\ell_{ba,a^{-1}b^{\prime}}\quad\forall a\in\mathcal{T}~.

(D.8)

Notice that one can also fuse the lines in
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
to lines on the interface
\mathcal{I}_{+-}
, with the fusion rules

\ell_{b_{1}}^{+}\otimes\ell_{b_{2},b_{3}}=\ell_{b_{1}b_{2},b_{3}}~,\quad\ell_{b_{2},b_{3}}\otimes\ell_{b_{1}}^{-}=\ell_{b_{2},b_{3}b_{1}}~.

(D.9)

Finally, the interface
\mathcal{I}_{+-}
will be a multi-fusion category, namely it contains local operators (corresponding to the multiple junctions between the identity lines), if
\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}
is non-trivial. And the local operators are exactly labeled by
\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}
, corresponding to the lines stretching between two interfaces. When this happens, it usually means the 2d interface contains a sub-sector described by a decoupled 2d TQFT.

Finally, one also needs to remember to keep track of whether the resulting 2d interface
\mathcal{I}_{-+}
couples to the bulk gauge field
a^{(2)}
. When it does, this will turn the decoupled 2d TQFT to a 2d condensation defect of the 1-form symmetry.

Appendix E
Duality defect associators

In this section, we briefly explain the non-invertible duality symmetry in 4d Maxwell theory at
\tau=iN
found in Ref.
[
9
,
12
]
and compute one particular associator of the duality defects using the Lagrangian method and group-theoretical approach. Other associators can be computed using the same methods and the results are listed. We find an agreement and comment on the efficiency and validity of the two approaches.

E.1
Non-invertible duality symmetry and defect Lagrangians

At couplings
\tau=iN
, there is a duality symmetry constructed as follows. We first gauge a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
subgroup of the electric 1-form symmetry in half space (with Dirichlet boundary conditions for the corresponding discrete gauge field), resulting in a topological interface between the theory with coupling
\tau=iN
and the theory with coupling
i/N
. However, since under
\mathbb{S}
-duality the coupling
i/N
maps to
iN
, the two sides are in fact equivalent and we get a defect in a single theory, obtained by performing
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{(1)}
gauging and
\mathbb{S}
-duality in half space. As shown in
[
9
]
, one can describe this defect, which is denoted by
D_{2}
, by the following action,

D_{2}:\quad S=\frac{N}{4\pi}\int_{x<0}dA_{L}\wedge\star dA_{L}+\frac{N}{4\pi}\int_{x>0}dA_{R}\wedge\star dA_{R}+\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{x=0}A_{L}\wedge dA_{R}\,

(E.1)

where
A_{L}
and
A_{R}
denote the Maxwell gauge field from the two sides of the defect, which is located at
x=0
.

The corresponding orientation reversal defect, denoted by
\overline{D}_{2}
, is given by

\overline{D}_{2}:\quad S=\frac{N}{4\pi}\int_{x<0}dA_{L}\wedge\star dA_{L}+\frac{N}{4\pi}\int_{x>0}dA_{R}\wedge\star dA_{R}-\frac{iN}{2\pi}\int_{x=0}A_{R}\wedge dA_{L}~.

(E.2)

Together and with the condensation defect
C_{0}
, they satisfy the non-invertible fusion algebra

\begin{gathered}D_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=C_{0}=\overline{D}_{2}\times D_{2},\quad D_{2}\times D_{2}=U\times C_{0}=\overline{D}_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2},\\
D_{2}\times C_{0}=C_{0}\times D_{2}=D_{2},\quad\overline{D}_{2}\times C_{0}=C_{0}\times\overline{D}_{2}=\overline{D}_{2}\end{gathered}

(E.3)

where
U
is the charge conjugation defect given by

U:\quad\frac{N}{4\pi}\int_{x<0}dA_{L}\wedge\star dA_{L}+\frac{N}{4\pi}\int_{x>0}dA_{R}\wedge\star dA_{R}+\frac{i}{2\pi}\int db\wedge(A_{L}+A_{R})~.

(E.4)

Let us first analyze the structure of the
F
-symbol by the number of incoming invertible charge conjugation defects
U
. First, when there are two incoming
U
defects, then the global fusion in the middle contains no decoupled TQFT or condensation defect supported on the interval; hence the
F
-symbol must be the trivial theory. Second, when there is one incoming
U
defect and two non-invertible defects, then the global fusion in the middle always contains a condensation defect supported on the interval; under the interval reduction, this always leads to a 2d condensation of the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry. Thus, we can now focus on the cases where all three incoming twist defects are non-invertible symmetries. We find

\left[F^{\overline{D}_{2}}_{D_{2},D_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{U,U},\quad\left[F^{D_{2}}_{\overline{D}_{2},D_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},U},\quad\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}},\quad\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},D_{2},\overline{D}_{2}}\right]_{U,\mathbb{1}}=\text{Trivial TQFT}~,

(E.5)

and the remaining ones are related to the above ones via orientation reversal. Below, we will only demonstrate explicitly the computation for
\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}
using the Lagrangian method since the other ones can be computed similarly. Notice that unlike the triality case, here the approach holds for all
N
.

E.2
Lagrangian method for
\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}

In this subsection, we compute the associator 1-morphism
\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}
for any
N
using the Lagrangian method in Section
4.3.1
.
To obtain
\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}
, we collapse the diamond-shaped region in Figure
20
to a 2d associator manifold. We begin by analysing each fusion/splitting and morphism junctions.

D_{2}
{D}_{2}~
~\mathbb{1},a
\overline{D}_{2}~
~{D}_{2}
\mathbb{1},b
D_{2}
A_{L}
A_{R}
A_{I}^{\prime}
A_{I}
\alpha

\beta^{\prime}

.

\beta
:
D_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=C_{0}

The action along the fused part is

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\beta}\left(-A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{R}\right)

(E.6)

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\beta}\mathrm{d}(-A_{I}^{\prime})\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})+{iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}}A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge(A_{R}-A_{I})

where
\int_{\beta}^{\infty}
is the 3d integral above the
\beta
junction and
\Sigma_{\beta}
is the 2d manifold supporting the
\beta
junction. Therefore, at this junction, the boundary term is

\beta:\quad\text{No boundary condition},\quad S(\beta)={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}}A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge(A_{R}-A_{I})~.

(E.7)

\beta^{\prime}:C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}
junction

Since we are interested in the trivial element of the Witt class, we add another junction
\beta^{\prime}
between
C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}
. The corresponding action is

S={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta^{\prime}}_{\beta}\mathrm{d}(-A_{I}^{\prime})\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\beta}\mathrm{d}a\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})~.

(E.8)

Using the analysis in Section
4.2.2
, especially Eq. (
4.21
), we impose the boundary condition and add the boundary term

\beta^{\prime}:\quad\left.a+NA_{I}^{\prime}\right|_{\beta^{\prime}}~,\quad\left.A_{I}-A_{R}\right|_{\beta^{\prime}}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge},\quad S(\beta^{\prime})=0~.

(E.9)

\alpha
:
D_{2}\times\mathbb{1}\to D_{2}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={i\over 2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\alpha}NA_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{I}+\mathrm{d}a(A_{I}-A_{R})

(E.10)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\alpha}(a+NA_{L})\mathrm{d}(A_{I}-A_{R})}_{\text{decoupled trivial TQFT}}+\underbrace{{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\infty}_{\alpha}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{R}}_{D_{2}}\underbrace{-{i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}a\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})}_{S(\alpha)}~.

Following the discussion in Section
4.2.1
, the decoupled trivial TQFT fields are condensed on the boundary. Hence,

\alpha:\quad\left.a+NA_{L}\right|_{\alpha},\quad\left.A_{I}-A_{R}\right|_{\alpha}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge},\quad S(\alpha)=-{i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}a\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R})~.

(E.11)

\gamma
:
C_{0}\to D_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}

The fusion outcome is
C_{0}
without other terms.

S={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\gamma}_{-\infty}(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}A_{I}~.

(E.12)

\gamma^{\prime}
:
\mathbb{1}\to C_{0}

S_{d}={i\over 2\pi}\int^{\gamma}_{\gamma^{\prime}}N\mathrm{d}A_{I}(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})+{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\gamma^{\prime}}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}b(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})~.

(E.13)

Again, using Eq. (
4.21
),

\gamma^{\prime}:\quad\left.A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime},\quad b-NA_{I}\right|_{\gamma^{\prime}}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge},\quad S(\gamma^{\prime})=0~.

(E.14)

\delta
:
D_{2}\to\mathbb{1}\times D_{2}

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={i\over 2\pi}\int^{\delta}_{-\infty}\left[\mathrm{d}b(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})+NA_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{R}\right]

(E.15)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{{i\over 2\pi}\int^{\delta}_{-\infty}(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}(b-NA_{R})}_{\text{decoupled trivial TQFT}}+\underbrace{{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\delta}_{-\infty}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{R}}_{D_{2}}~.

The boundary conditions are

\delta:\quad\left.A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}\quad b-NA_{R}\right|_{\delta}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge},\quad S(\delta)=0~.

(E.16)

Merging the upper and lower junctions

As the next step, we collapse all the upper junctions into one and combine the boundary data, and similarly for the lower junctions. We get

\displaystyle\alpha,\beta,\beta^{\prime}:

\displaystyle a+NA_{L},\quad a+NA_{I}^{\prime},\quad A_{I}-A_{R}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}

(E.17)

\displaystyle S_{\alpha}=-{i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha}}a\wedge(A_{I}-A_{R}),\quad S_{\beta}={iN\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\beta}}A_{I}^{\prime}\wedge(A_{R}-A_{I})

\displaystyle\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},\delta:

\displaystyle b=NA_{I},\quad A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime},\quad b-NA_{R}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}

\displaystyle S_{\gamma}=S_{\gamma^{\prime}}=S_{\delta}=0~.

Global fusion

Next, we fuse the three defects
D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}
in the middle (Figure
21
).

{D}_{2}
\overline{D}_{2}
D_{2}
A_{L}
A_{I}
A_{I}^{\prime}
A_{R}

Figure 21
:
Fusing
{D}_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}
at
[\gamma,\beta]
.

The corresponding action is the sum of these defect actions, which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle={iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}A_{I}+A_{I}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}A_{R}

(E.18)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}(A_{I}-A_{R})}_{\mathbb{Z}_{N}\text{ gauge theory}}+\underbrace{{iN\over 2\pi}\int^{\beta}_{\gamma}A_{L}\mathrm{d}A_{R}}_{D_{2}}~.

The first term corresponds to the
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory. Collecting the boundary terms at the upper junctions,

\displaystyle S(\alpha)+S(\beta)

\displaystyle={i\over 2\pi}\int_{\Sigma_{\alpha\beta}}(a+NA_{I}^{\prime})\wedge(A_{R}-A_{I})\in 2\pi i\mathbb{Z}~,

(E.19)

where we used the boundary conditions
a+NA_{I}^{\prime}
and
A_{R}-A_{I}
being pure
U(1)
gauge at the upper junction. And the total boundary term at the lower junction simply vanishes. The boundary conditions of each conjugate are

\displaystyle\alpha\beta:

\displaystyle N(A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime})=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\,

\displaystyle(N),

\displaystyle A_{I}-A_{R}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}

\displaystyle(D)~,

(E.20)

\displaystyle\gamma\delta:

\displaystyle A_{L}-A_{I}^{\prime}=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}\,

\displaystyle(D),

\displaystyle N(A_{I}-A_{R})=\text{pure $U(1)$ gauge}

\displaystyle(N)~.

Following the discussion in Sections
4.3.1
, there is no untwisted local operator, and hence the 2d effective theory for the 3d
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory on the interval with the above boundary conditions is a trivial theory. Thus we find

\left[F_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}^{D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}=\text{Trivial TQFT}~.

(E.21)

E.3
Group-theoretical approach

E.3.1
Group theoretical construction

For some special values of
N
, the non-invertible duality can be constructed group-theoretically
[
32
,
12
,
14
]
. In particular, if
N
is odd
and
if
\exists~p
such that
p^{2}=-1
mod
N
37
37

37

Notice that when
N
is odd and such
p
exists, we can always and will choose
p
to be even.
, the non-invertible duality defect in a QFT
\mathcal{X}
becomes invertible after the
ST
gauging with the following discrete torsion.

Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[C^{(2)}]=\sum_{c^{(2)}\in H^{2}(M_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{N})}Z_{\mathcal{X}}[c^{(2)}]~e^{{2\pi i\over N}\int_{M_{4}}\left({N+1\over 2}p~c^{(2)}\cup c^{(2)}+c^{(2)}\cup C^{(2)}\right)}

(E.22)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
is the QFT obtained by the above
ST
gauging in QFT
\mathcal{X}
and
C^{(2)}
is the 2-form background field of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry. Utilising the duality

Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[C^{(2)}]=\sum_{c^{(2)}\in H^{2}(M_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{N})}Z_{\mathcal{X}}[c^{(2)}]~e^{{2\pi i\over N}\int_{M_{4}}c^{(2)}\cup C^{(2)}}~,

(E.23)

it follows that

Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[C^{(2)}]=Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[pC^{(2)}]~e^{{2\pi i\over N}{N+1\over 2}p\int_{M_{4}}C^{(2)}\cup C^{(2)}}~.

(E.24)

This implies that the non-invertible duality defect in
\mathcal{X}
is mapped to a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}^{(0)}
invertible
0
-form symmetry in the theory
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, which is an automorphism of the dual
1
-form symmetry sending
C^{(2)}\mapsto pC^{(2)}
. The additional phase in (
E.24
) represents the mixed anomaly between the
0
-form symmetry and the
1
-form symmetry. Let
\mathbb{D}
be the generator of the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}^{(0)}
symmetry. As for the triality case, we dress the minimal TQFT to make
\mathbb{D}
a gauge invariant operator, consistent of gauging the dual 1-form symmetry in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
,

D_{2}=\mathbb{D}_{2}\mathcal{A}^{N,p}[c^{(2)}]

(E.25)

where
c
is the gauge field for the bulk 1-form symmetry. Applying the relation (
E.25
) twice

Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[C^{(2)}]=Z_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}[-C^{(2)}]~,

(E.26)

we see that
\mathbb{D}^{2}
acts as charge conjugation of the dual
1
-form symmetry without a mixed anomaly in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
. Finally, the orientation reversal of
D_{2}
is given by

\overline{D}_{2}=\mathcal{A}^{N,-p}[c^{(2)}]{\mathbb{D}}^{-1}~.

(E.27)

It is straightforward to check the above defects do reproduce the generic fusion rules (
E.3
) following a similar approach in the triality case.

We can then proceed to work out the local fusion junctions and compute the
F
-symbols, and we find the results agree with the Lagrangian method. We will skip the details here, and only briefly list the results. For simplicity, we will choose
N=5
(and take
p=2
), but it is completely straightforward to acquire the result for the generic cases. With this choice,

D_{2}=\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A}^{5,2}[c^{(2)}],\quad\overline{D}_{2}=\mathcal{A}^{5,-2}[c^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}^{-1}

(E.28)

and the invertible 0-form symmetry
\mathbb{D}
acts on the 1-form symmetry operators as the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
automorphism

\mathbb{D}\mathcal{A}^{5,\pm 2}[c^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}^{-1}=\mathcal{A}^{5,\pm 2}[-2c^{(2)}]~,\quad\mathbb{D}^{-1}\mathcal{A}^{5,\pm 2}[c^{(2)}]\mathbb{D}=\mathcal{A}^{5,\pm 2}[2c^{(2)}]~.

(E.29)

E.3.2
Fusion and condensation

The 3d
\mathbb{Z}_{5}
gauge theory with 0-twist coupled to the external dynamical 1-form
\mathbb{Z}_{5}
gauge field
c\in H^{2}(M_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{5})
with the following Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{(\mathcal{Z}_{5})_{0}}[c^{(2)}]=\frac{2\pi i}{5}a^{(1)}\cup\delta b^{(1)}+\frac{2\pi i}{5}a^{(1)}\wedge c^{(2)}

(E.30)

represents the condensation defect
C_{0}
in the path integral. To see this, we integrate
b^{(1)}
out to limit
a\in H^{1}(M_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{5})
and replace
a^{(1)}
with its Poincaré dual
\int_{M_{3}}a^{(1)}\cup\cdots=\int_{\Sigma_{2}}\cdots
in the integral. The path integral is then

\sum_{a\in H^{1}(M_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{5})}e^{\frac{2\pi i}{5}\int_{M_{3}}a^{(1)}\cup c^{(2)}}=\sum_{\Sigma_{2}\in H_{2}(M_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{5})}e^{{2\pi i\over 5}\oint_{\Sigma_{2}}c^{(2)}}=\sum_{i,\{m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{5}\}}e^{{2\pi i\over 5}m_{i}\oint_{\Sigma_{2,i}}c^{(2)}}

(E.31)

where
\{\Sigma_{2,i}\}
are basis of the non-contractible 2-cycles on
M_{3}
, and we normalised
c
in the integral so that the 1-form charge is integer quantised. In this form, the path integral is manifestly the 1-form symmetry condensation defect.

If one condenses
a
by restricting to a trivial cohomology element in the path integral, we see that the constrained path integral is trivial, i.e. it is the identity defect. Using this, the morphism
C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}
is implemented by condensing
a
at the morphism junction (and along
\mathbb{1}
).

With all necessary ingredients at hand, we discuss the anyon condensation at the local fusion and morphism junctions.

D_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}

Consider a fusion

\displaystyle D_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=\mathcal{A}^{5,2}_{a_{1}}[-2c^{(2)}]\otimes\mathcal{A}^{5,-2}_{a_{2}}[-2c^{(2)}]

(E.32)

where we used the
\mathbb{D}
action on the minimal TQFTs (
E.28
), and
a_{1,2}
denotes the generating anyons of the minimal TQFT in each theory. To see if the theory on the RHS is the condensation defect
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
gauge theory (
E.30
), we first note that
a=(a_{1})^{2}(a_{2})^{2}
is coupled to the external field
c^{(2)}
as in (
E.30
) and has the topological spin
h[a]=0
. This means that it is enough to find
b
such that
a
and
b
have the correct topological spin and braiding to be the pure electric line and the pure magnetic line in
(\mathcal{Z}_{N})_{0}
. Let
b=(a_{1})^{m}(a_{2})^{n}
. Then
h[b]=0
and
B[a,b]=\exp\left({2\pi i\over 5}\right)
for
b=(a_{1})^{-2}(a_{2})^{2}
. Hence the fusion outcome is
C_{0}
as expected. Then the morphism junction
C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}
is obtained by condensing the Wilson lines in
\langle(a_{1})^{-2}(a_{2})^{-2}\rangle=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle
, i.e.

D_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}\quad\text{by condensing $\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle$}~.

(E.33)

\overline{D}_{2}\times{D}_{2}=C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}

Following the same discussion, we can confirm the fusion algebra
\overline{D}_{2}\times D_{2}=C_{0}
with
a=a_{1}a_{2}
and
b=a_{1}(a_{2})^{-1}
. Hence, at the morphism junction,

\overline{D}_{2}\times{D}_{2}=C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}\quad\text{by condensing $\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle$}~.

(E.34)

D_{2}\times D_{2}=U\times C_{0}\to U

This time, consider the fusion

\displaystyle D_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=\mathbb{D}^{2}\mathcal{A}^{5,2}_{a_{1}}[2c^{(2)}]\otimes\mathcal{A}^{5,2}_{a_{2}}[c^{(2)}]~.

(E.35)

We note that
\mathbb{D}^{2}=U
is the charge conjugation. Following the same argument, the minimal TQFT part corresponds to
C_{0}
with
a=(a_{1})^{2}a_{2}
and
b=(a_{1})^{2}(a_{2})^{-1}
. Again,
C_{0}\to\mathbb{1}
is done by condensing
\langle(a_{1})^{2}a_{2}\rangle
.

D_{2}\times D_{2}=U\times C_{0}\to U\quad\text{by condensing $\langle(a_{1})^{2}a_{2}\rangle$}~.

(E.36)

\overline{D}_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=U\times C_{0}\to U

The above discussion straightforwardly applies to this fusion and morphism junctions, with
a=a_{1}(a_{2})^{2}
and
b=(a_{1})^{-2}a_{2}
.

\overline{D}_{2}\times\overline{D}_{2}=U\times C_{0}\to U\quad\text{by condensing $\langle a_{1}(a_{2})^{2}\rangle$}~.

(E.37)

E.3.3
Associator

In this subsection, we compute the associator 1-morphism for the three defects, say
1,2,3
, by first collapsing the upper junctions
(23)
and
1(23)
into
\mathcal{I}_{+}
and similarly for the lower junctions (first arrow in Figure
22
), and finally reducing the interval to merge
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}\to\mathcal{I}
. At this final step, we identify the resulting 2d theory on the associator 1-morphism using the spectrum of the local and line operators.

1(23)
(23)
(12)
(12)3
\to
\mathcal{I}_{+}
\mathbb{A}_{+}
\mathcal{I}_{-}
\mathbb{A}_{-}
\to
\mathcal{I}

Figure 22
:
Anyon condensation in the associator 1-morphism of defects
1,2,3

To be specific, let’s first denote by
\mathbb{A}_{ij}
the condensed anyons at the local fusion
i\times j
junction. For example, anyons in
\mathbb{A}_{23}
are condensed at the junction
(23)
and above, and anyons in
\mathbb{A}_{12}
are condensed at the junction
(12)
and below. After merging the two upper junctions
1(23)
and
(23)
into
\mathcal{I}_{+}
, the anyons in
\mathbb{A}_{+}=\mathbb{A}_{1(23)}\times\mathbb{A}_{23}
are condensed and similarly at the lower junctions with
\mathbb{A}_{-}=\mathbb{A}_{12}\times\mathbb{A}_{(12)3}
.

The lines that are condensed at both
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
(i.e.
\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}
) stretch between the two junctions. Hence, after merging
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}\to\mathcal{I}
, they become a vertex operator.

The line operators on
\mathcal{I}
are the lines on three defects after quotient out
\mathbb{A}_{+}
and
\mathbb{A}_{-}
. The remaining lines can be in the 3d minimal TQFT coupling to the bulk fields
c^{(2)}
, which are neutral under
\mathbb{A}_{+}\times\mathbb{A}_{-}
. Quotient out those lines, we then acquire the lines in the decoupled 2d theory.

In summary, the spectrum of the decoupled 2d theory on
\mathcal{I}
after the reduction is

\displaystyle\text{Vertex operators}:

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}

(E.38)

\displaystyle\text{Line operators}:

\displaystyle(\text{neutral lines under $\mathbb{A}_{+}\times\mathbb{A}_{-}$})\backslash\mathbb{Z}_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}_{5}\times\mathbb{Z}_{5}/(\mathbb{A}_{+}\times\mathbb{A}_{-})

Example:
\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}

Using Eqs. (
E.33
) and (
E.34
), we identify

\mathbb{A}_{23}=\langle a_{2}a_{3}\rangle,\quad\mathbb{A}_{1(23)}=0,\quad\mathbb{A}_{12}=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle,\quad\mathbb{A}_{(12)3}=0

(E.39)

and hence

\mathbb{A}_{+}=\langle a_{2}a_{3}\rangle,\quad\mathbb{A}_{-}=\langle a_{1}a_{2}\rangle~.

(E.40)

Since
\mathbb{A}_{+}\cap\mathbb{A}_{-}=0
, there is no vertex operator on
\mathcal{I}
. The lines that are neutral under
\mathbb{A}_{+}\times\mathbb{A}_{-}
are those in
\langle a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\rangle
which has no overlap with
\mathbb{A}_{+}\times\mathbb{A}_{-}
, the lines condensed on
\mathcal{I}
. Therefore, there is no line left on the 2d theory in the double coset (
E.38
). In other words,

\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}}=\text{Trivial TQFT}~.

(E.41)

This is consistent with the result using the Lagrangian method (
E.21
) with
N=5
.

Other associators

Using the same procedures, one can compute the other associators, which are all trivial TQFT, matching the results of the Lagrangian method,

\left[F^{\overline{D}_{2}}_{D_{2},D_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{U,U},\quad\left[F^{D_{2}}_{\overline{D}_{2},D_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},U},\quad\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},\overline{D}_{2},D_{2}}\right]_{\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}},\quad\left[F^{D_{2}}_{D_{2},D_{2},\overline{D}_{2}}\right]_{U,\mathbb{1}}=\text{Trivial TQFT}~.

(E.42)

Lagrangian vs group-theoretical methods

While the group-theoretical approach is simpler than the Lagrangian method, it is not available for some
N
(of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
1-form symmetry) while the Lagrangian method applies to all
N
.
